“Your brother is not the first individual with a reduced reflex to be used in a time of crisis. During the Cleansing, such individuals entered nests, led predators into traps and stayed beside unstable machinery. Afterwards, almost all their lines were reproductively discouraged.”

“Why?”

“Because a society may need a few who do not run. It cannot trust an entire population that does not know when to.”

Sava felt anger rising in her throat.

“The Chamber still screens out the trait.”

“The Chamber screens out many biological memories of the age when we killed one another.”

“And now you’re sending Taren to do exactly the thing for which you prevented his line from continuing.”

“I did not build the contradiction. I merely recognised it in time.”

• • •

On returning to the inhabited levels, Sava found Daran Veyr waiting in the provincial audience hall. He was a head taller than the Custodian and wore Vaor’s brown cloak, reinforced at the edges with plates of fossil wood. For four centuries, the Houses had been forced to leave their old armour in museums, but each had turned its clothing into subtle memories of it.

Daran stood in the centre of the hall, exposed, ignoring the cultural reflex to remain close to the walls. When he saw her, he turned his whole body towards her.

“Sava Ilen.”

It was not a question.

“Lord Veyr.”

“Yesterday your brother did something ugly and necessary.”

“He did something necessary. The ugliness came before.”

Daran briefly showed his chisel teeth, a provincial smile too broad for the capital’s taste.

“In Vaor, the distinction between before and after does not feed us.”

“You’ll have the chance to show me the data.”

“The data are the corpses of the roots. You may count them.”

“That is what I intend.”

“And Taren?”

Sava noticed that he had not asked whether Taren was coming, but was already laying claim to him.

“Taren decides for himself.”

Daran Veyr did not leave after their brief conversation. He followed Sava to the exit gallery, keeping the polite distance of two arm’s lengths. His guards — officially, ranking servants — moved without a sound, though they wore heavy footwear. In Ivarra, only those who wanted to conceal vibrations learned to walk like that.

“You’ve seen the archive,” he said.

Sava stopped.

“I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

“The smell of preservative stays in the fur. Beneath the stone, the capital keeps precisely the things for which it denies us weapons.”

“What are you really asking for, Lord Veyr?”

“The right for my people not to die administratively.”

“A beautiful turn of phrase doesn’t turn a guard into an irrigation channel.”

“Nor does an official one turn hunger into a deferral.”

Daran came close enough for Sava to see the small notches in his chisel teeth. In the old provinces, fighters marked every kill. These could have been decorative; that was precisely why they were unsettling.

“Vaor has been losing roots for six years,” he continued. “We report the larvae, the changing humidity, the thefts and the collapse of the replacement organisms. The Great Hearing replies that the Belt’s total yield compensates. The centre calls it peace when death happens far enough away.”

“And a saryx in Ivarra brings it closer.”

For the first time, his smile disappeared.

“You think I released it.”

“I think you benefit.”

“Benefit is not evidence. Otherwise, the Chamber of Measure would be guilty of every child not born.”

He bowed very slightly.

“Come and count us. But count what the system has decided does not deserve a number too.”

• • •

In the family apartment, Taren was packing the few things he owned: two uniforms, a tool for sharpening his chisel teeth, a reading slate, the black fragment he should have handed over to the laboratory. Nemi sat on the bed, watching him. The tip of one sail had been stitched and held in a transparent splint.

“Mother says Vaor is cold,” she said.

“Mother tells the truth when she talks about temperatures.”

“Are there monsters there?”

“They’re everywhere. They usually hold office.”

Nemi thought about it.

“What office do you hold?”

Taren fastened his bag.

“They haven’t invented my name yet.”

Sava came in in time to hear.

“You don’t have to come.”

“The Custodian said I do.”

“I can challenge the appointment.”

“Why would you?”

“Because Seln is using you as evidence. Daran will use you as a flag. Neither of them is interested in you.”

“Yesterday no one was interested in me. It was simpler, not better.”

Oru leaned against the doorframe. He did not intervene. Taren touched Nemi’s splint with an unexpected gentleness in his bandaged fingers.

“When the shadows came,” he said, “everyone knew what they had to do. I didn’t. For a moment, I was the only free person in the square.”

“You were the only defective one,” Sava replied.

“Perhaps freedom is the beautiful name we give a defect when it becomes useful.”

He lifted his bag and walked past her.

“We leave on the first Night train.”

“Taren.”

He stopped.

Sava wanted to tell him about the three incubators, the identity of a dead man and the hidden tanks. She wanted to tell him that the province was waiting for him too eagerly for the meeting to be accidental. Instead of all that, she asked:

“What did you feel when you killed the creature?”

Taren kept his back to her. His sails moved slowly, listening to the room, the pipes, the whole city.

“That the world had stopped lying to me,” he said.

After Taren left, Sava stayed in his room. The bed had not been made. In the open drawer she found his old therapy records, which he had kept despite claiming not to care about the diagnosis. Each showed the same graph: stimulus, delay, reaction. At seven, the delay had been 0.8 seconds; at twelve, 0.5; in recent months, 0.4. An entire life reduced to the interval in which the others ran before him.

Oru came in and closed the drawer.

“You shouldn’t read that without him.”

“I signed for the treatments.”

“Then you’ve read them enough.”

Sava sat on the edge of the bed.

“Seln knew. They used people like Taren during the Cleansing, then reduced their reproductive rights.”

“It isn’t surprising.”

“It should be.”

“It should,” Oru said. “But systems keep the tools they condemn. They give them another name and lock them away until they need them.”

“He’s coming with us.”

“I know.”

“And Nemi.”

Oru’s sails rose abruptly.

“No.”

“The school gardens are closing for the inquiry. I can’t leave her with relatives; half the city is under investigation, and the other half is spreading rumours of saryxes in the pipes.”

“Then I’ll stay with her.”

“We need you in Vaor. Agricultural consumption and the root network are the key to the data.”

“The Custodian has thousands of engineers.”

“None I trust.”

Silence tightened between them. Nemi appeared in the doorway, carrying her small travel bag. She had heard enough.

“Is Vaor cold?” she asked.

Oru closed his eyes for a moment.

“Yes.”

“Are there monsters?”

Sava answered before he could:

“There are problems we don’t understand yet.”

“Taren says monsters hold office.”

“Taren says things that sound good and are hard to use.”

Nemi put her bag on the bed.

“Then I’m coming to see what you use.”

Before bed, Nemi began saying goodbye to the apartment. She did not call it that. She went from room to room, recording the sounds she knew on her slate: the pump on level four, the vibration of the neighbours’ lift, the crack of the load-bearing root when the orbital mirror changed the angle of the light. For each recording, she said the place and time, like a very small technician.

“You can’t take the house with you,” Sava told her.

“I’m not taking the house. I’m taking what the house does when no one is talking.”

Oru, kneeling beside the service wall, was detaching their personal connections from the root network. The living strands withdrew slowly into niches, leaving transparent droplets behind. The apartment would be allocated to another family before their train reached Vaor. In Ivarra, space did not remain sentimental.

Nemi went into her room and pulled a box of useless objects from beneath the bed: a red stone from the surface, two dried plant membranes, a festival figurine and the milk tooth the Chamber had requested for the genetic archive, but which Oru had declared lost.

“Only what fits in the bag,” Sava said.

The child placed the box beside the luggage.

“It fits if I take out the clothes.”

“It’s cold in Vaor.”

“Then I’ll wear all the clothes.”

Oru laughed, then stopped, surprised by his own sound. It was the first time any of them had laughed since the square. Nemi looked at both of them, checking whether it was allowed. Sava felt how quickly children turned their parents’ state into natural law.

“Take the stone and the figurine,” she said. “The membranes will crumble. The tooth stays here.”

“Why?”

“Because it belongs to the archive.”

“Then do I belong to the archive too?”

Sava opened her mouth and closed it. Oru took the tooth from the box and wrapped it in a piece of cloth.

“She’s taking it,” he said.

“It’s undeclared genetic material.”

“It’s a piece of our child.”

“That’s exactly why the rules exist.”

“The rules exist for samples, not memories.”

“You can’t separate the two.”

Nemi held the bag open between them. The argument had not grown louder, but her sails followed every change in tone.

Oru put the tooth in the inner pocket.

“Then let someone record that I chose wrong.”

Sava wanted to force him to leave it. It would have been simple: a notification, a departure inventory, an agent on the platform. Instead, she sat beside Nemi and folded the clothes so that there would be room for the stone.

“Record our room too,” she said.

Nemi switched on the slate. For a few moments, all three were silent. They could hear the root strands withdrawing from the wall, their breathing and, very far away, the mourning blows still travelling through the city. The apartment was not quiet. It never had been. Until then, they had simply known the names of all its noises.

• • •

A few minutes after mid-cycle, the Great Hearing issued an automatic correction to Vaor’s census. The estimated population was not four hundred and seventy thousand, but five hundred and twenty-three thousand.

Sava opened the data’s provenance layer. The correction had been triggered by enormous nocturnal consumption in twenty-seven agricultural chambers declared abandoned.

The pattern did not resemble the feeding of adults.

Sava projected the consumption pattern over the map of the abandoned chambers. The pulses were short and frequent, clustered around the hours when the automated inspections recalibrated their sensors. A hidden population knew not only how to live outside the registers, but when the system blinked.

At the edge of the model, a second anomaly appeared: small quantities of protein transported from Vaor to Ivarra over the past eight months. The destinations had been erased, but the accumulated mass corresponded to the three incubators.

“Someone is feeding children and predators through the same hole,” Oru said.

Sava looked at the two superimposed patterns. She did not yet know whether they were the work of the same network or whether one injustice had been used to conceal another.

On the room’s wall, the departure time lit up in white.

NIGHT TRAIN · 03:10 · FOUR AUTHORISED PASSENGERS.

Below the list appeared a note added by the Custodian’s office:

SECURITY OFFICER: TAREN ILEN.

For the first time, her brother’s defect held an official position.


PART II

USEFUL MONSTERS

• • •


CHAPTER VI

THE NIGHT TRAIN

SAVA · THE ROAD BETWEEN IVARRA AND VAOR

The trains towards the edge of the Night had been built to make distance bearable. Passengers spent most of the journey beneath rock and saw the world only when they could no longer get off.

The Night Train left Ivarra without windows.

As far as the border of Vaor, the tunnel followed the safe depth, where rock retained heat and no traveller had to see the surface. Only after thirteen hours did the carriages rise towards the Belt, and their walls become transparent. Beyond them, Arua unfolded like a wound between two infinities.

To the east, the Day was a reddish-white plain beneath shimmering air. To the west, the Night began under a wall of blue clouds, its glaciers faintly reflecting the light of the orbital mirrors. Between them stretched the low forests of mineral ferns, the black channels and the mounds of buried cities. The star Vei never rose or set. It hung at the same height, a red coin driven into the horizon.

The nivari in the carriage instinctively pressed towards the side away from the window. Even after centuries of safety, the open surface quickened their hearts. Sava too felt the impulse to crouch. Taren stood straight, looking at the sky.

“Doesn’t it bother you?” Oru asked.

“What?”

“The fact that there’s no ceiling.”

Taren raised a hand towards the glass.

“There is. It’s just very far away.”

Nemi laughed. Sava had not wanted to take her, but the Chamber of Measure estimated the inspection would last three months, and the capital’s Children’s Gardens had been closed after the attack. Oru had come officially as a root-network engineer, unofficially because he refused to leave his family divided after almost losing it in the square.

In the family compartment, Nemi had built a shelter from two blankets and Taren’s bag. She did not want to sleep, but she did not want to see the door either. Each time the train changed speed, she pressed her palm to the floor and asked what came next.

“A compensation node,” Oru would answer.

“How do you know?”

“The vibration comes from both sides. The brakes are only at the front.”

“And now?”

“A side pump.”

“And now?”

“Taren is chewing too loudly.”

A sound that might have been laughter came from the shelter. Taren stopped chewing his dried root and put it aside.

Sava worked at the folding table. The Great Hearing had sent her Vaor’s files in separate layers: official population, estimated consumption, food requests, deaths, migrations. The data did not match even when she excluded the abandoned chambers. The hidden children left traces in everything they consumed, but appeared in none of what they were granted.

“Eighty thousand?” Oru asked, looking over her shoulder.

“At least. Perhaps a hundred and twenty.”

“How do you hide one city inside another?”

“You don’t hide it from the people who live there. You hide it from the one counting at a distance.”

Taren turned one sail towards them.

“And what if Daran isn’t lying about the harvests?”

“He lied about the people,” Sava said.

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It’s a reason to check everything.”

Oru touched the network map. Vaor’s main pipes appeared as thin veins, many with low yields.

“The loss can’t come only from the extra bodies. The flow drops before it reaches the inhabited areas.”

“The animals Daran was talking about?”

“Or diseased roots. Or theft. Or someone is diverting nutrient into the chambers declared empty.”

Taren picked up his food again.

“The same explanation can feed both children and saryxes.”

Sava stopped working. Nemi was not meant to hear. From the shelter, the little girl said:

“I know what a saryx is.”

“That is precisely the problem,” Sava replied.

At the change of cycle, a servant of House Veyr brought dinner and a message: Daran was inviting Taren to the provincial carriage. Taren declined. Ten minutes later, Daran came himself.

The lord of Vaor did not seem offended by the cramped space. He sat on the floor between Oru’s tool bag and Nemi’s shelter, his stiff cloak taking up half the compartment.

“In my House,” he said, “a guest who refuses a meal suggests that the host wants to poison him.”

“In Ivarra, a lord who comes uninvited suggests the same thing,” Taren replied.

Daran laughed. Nemi poked her head between the blankets to look at him.

“Are you the one with the cold city?” she asked.

“I’m the one trying to keep it warm.”

“Mother says you lied about the children.”

Sava closed her eyes. Daran turned his attention to the little girl.

“Your mother is right.”

“Why?”

“Because the truth sent to Ivarra came back with less food than it had left with.”

“That doesn’t make sense.”

“You’ll discover that many adult things are made precisely so they won’t make sense to children.”

Sava intervened:

“Don’t use my child as a platform.”

“I’m not using her. She asks better questions than the inspectors.”

Daran held out a slate to Taren. The screen showed three attacks on Vaor’s transports: masked figures, thermal lights, chaotic flight. In the final frame, a worker remained on the floor.

“This one died a month ago,” Daran said. “No House agent fired. No thief had been killed before then. Something has changed.”

“The weapons,” Taren said.

“And the people using them.”

“Who supplied them?”

“If I knew, I wouldn’t have let you refuse the meal.”

Sava took the slate. The metadata had been scrubbed, but not completely. A series of identifiers came from the central inventory.

“These projectiles were manufactured in Ivarra.”

“Almost everything capable of hurting us is manufactured there,” Daran said. “Some of it under the name of medical instruments.”

Before leaving, he stopped beside Nemi’s shelter.

“When the train rises to the surface, don’t look only at the sky,” he told her. “Look at the line between light and darkness. That’s where we live.”

“Why don’t you build closer to the light?”

“Because it burns us.”

“Closer to the darkness?”

“Because it freezes us.”

Nemi thought about it.

“Then your city has nowhere to run.”

Daran looked at her a moment too long.

“Exactly.”

The train slowed shortly after Daran left, but the vibration was nothing like braking at a compensation node. First the regular rumble beneath the floor disappeared. Then a dull blow passed through the carriages, followed by a silence so complete that Sava heard the liquid moving in Nemi’s container.

The little girl came out of the shelter.

“What’s that?”

Oru pressed his fingers to the floor. He waited. Nothing.

“I don’t know.”

It was the first answer of that kind he had given her since they left. Nemi drew her blanket around her shoulders.

The lights switched to backup. The conductor’s voice came from the corridor, reassuring and prepared too quickly: a technical stop at Root Six, no danger, no access outside, the journey to resume after the ventilation was checked. Taren stood before the announcement ended.

“‘No danger’ means they haven’t decided whose it is yet,” he said.

The compartment door remained locked. Oru took a service blade from his bag, detached the control cover and pressed two contacts. The mechanism gave way with a guilty sound.

The corridor was crowded. Passengers from the centre kept their luggage close, their sails lowered so as not to touch anyone. In front of the freight carriage, three employees of House Veyr were trying to push back a group of people dressed in work cloth. They wore no passenger wristbands. A woman held a child against her abdomen, and a boy Nemi’s age pressed his sleeve over his mouth and coughed without a sound.

“Back into the agricultural compartment,” one of the employees ordered. “The ventilation is restarting.”

“That’s where it stopped,” the woman replied.

Oru walked past him and opened the freight door. The air inside was damp and heavy. Mesh beds had been stretched between crates of spores, filters and nutrient tubes. Twenty-seven people shared the space reserved, by volume, for sixteen tonnes of equipment. Condensation ran down the walls. An older man was trying to turn a jammed fan by hand.

“Stop it,” Oru said. “The motor’s overheated.”

“If we stop, the children have no air.”

“If you keep at it, the coil catches fire and no one does.”

Oru pulled out the control box. Behind the grille he found a thick felt of black dust, textile fibres and dried spores. The filter had not been changed for three cycles. Yet the label showed the last inspection signed two days earlier.

Sava opened the manifest. There was no group of travellers. The carriage was transporting “biological auxiliaries for crop restoration” and thirty-two units of “temporary manual capacity”.

“Units,” she read.

The woman with the child heard her.

“It’s cheaper than people.”

“Who registered you this way?”

“The station. Vaor sent work contracts, but Ivarra didn’t approve the migration. So we aren’t migrating. We’re being delivered.”

“Where are you from?”

“Vel Four. The southern mirror was moved, and the starch ferns died in two weeks. We were told to wait for redistribution. We waited until we’d eaten the seed.”

Sava searched for Vel Four. The region appeared stable, its production decline classified as normal variation. No report of evacuation. No unusual death. Only a small rise in “contractual mobility”, the precise term under which the people before her had disappeared from one community without appearing in another.

“The child has a fever,” Taren said.

He did not mean the one in the woman’s arms, but the coughing boy. The boy instinctively recoiled when Taren approached. His movement was so fast that he hit the wall and shielded his head before understanding that no one was attacking him.

Nemi held out her water container.

“I’m Nemi.”

The boy looked first at her hand, then her face, then the corridor behind her. His lateral field searched for an exit with every breath.

“Kol,” he said at last.

“How old are you?”

“Ten.”

He was only a year older than her and a head shorter. Nemi made no comment. She held the container until he drank.

The conductor appeared with Daran. The former wore an immaculate cloak and the expression of someone informed that reality was violating his procedure. Daran did not seem surprised.

“The compartment must be cleared,” the conductor said. “Passengers without status cannot enter the passenger carriages.”

“Then open the empty carriages,” Sava replied.

“They’re reserved for the provincial delegation.”

Daran raised a hand.

“The delegation can stand.”

“The safety regulations—”

“The safety regulations have just transported twenty-seven people without air,” Oru said. “Don’t invoke them again until I’ve repaired what they signed off on.”

The House employees began moving the families. Passengers from the centre stepped aside, but not all willingly. A woman drew her child close when Kol passed, as though hunger could be transmitted by touch. An old man protested that he had paid for quiet. Taren looked at him until he lowered his sails and fell silent.

Sava stayed beside the manifest with Daran.

“You call them freight.”

“I take them where there is food.”

“In a carriage that almost killed them.”

“The station saved on the filter. The House paid for a new one.”

“After falsifying the inspection.”

“An official falsified it so the transport wouldn’t be stopped. If he’d reported the fault, the train would have left without them.”

Sava looked at him. She hated the way every abuse in Vaor came with an explanation reasonable enough to divide the blame among victims, officials and distance.

“You could have requested a humanitarian route.”

“I requested six. The Chamber replied that Vel Four had no verified demographic surplus.”

“Because the people had been removed from the registers as ‘units’.”

“They were removed after the refusal. The order matters when you’re looking for the guilty. It doesn’t feed them when you find them.”

Oru called for a cooling kit. Daran went to fetch it himself. Sava did not know whether the gesture was useful or calculated to be seen. With him, the two possibilities never excluded each other.

The repair took almost two hours. Oru improvised a filter from the membranes of a germination crate, while Bes, the Vaor technician travelling in the provincial carriage, restored the power supply. Throughout, Kol stayed in the family compartment. Nemi showed him how to recognise pumps by their vibration. He showed her a code of knocks used in Vel Four when the dust made voices useless: two for water, three for danger, four slow ones for someone left behind.

“Where we live, four slow ones means the meal is ready,” Nemi said.

“Then where you live, no one gets left behind hungry.”

It was not a joke. Nemi did not laugh.

When the train started again, Kol and the others were allowed to remain in the passenger carriages. The conductor demanded Sava’s signature for the deviation. She signed and added the actual number of people. The system immediately flagged the discrepancy with the manifest.

A question appeared on the screen: *Do you wish to report a classification error?*

Sava pressed *yes*.

Then the second appeared: *Reporting may suspend transport pending clarification. Continue?*

She stopped. In the compartment, Kol had fallen asleep with Nemi’s container in his arms. The woman from Vel Four had covered him with her own cloak. If Sava continued, the train could be stopped at the next node and the people sent back to a place without a harvest. If she did not, their transport remained legal only because they had not been considered people.

She saved the report locally and deferred transmission.

It was exactly the kind of compromise she had condemned in others: truth kept in a drawer until it became safe. For the first time, she understood how little corruption, from within, resembled a clear choice.

• • •

When the walls became transparent, Nemi did not scream. She pulled the blanket over her head and refused to come out. Oru sat beside her and described what he saw without making her look: mineral ferns bending in the wind, black pipes, condensation towers, blue snow towards the Night. Taren remained at the window.

The sky made him feel not small, but unfinished. In Ivarra, every ceiling told you how much room there was and where the world ended. Here, nothing brought the gaze to an end. The red star did not move, and the shadows pointed forever in the same direction. Their ancestors had crossed this strip in short leaps, stopping only beneath roots or rocks. Taren felt their desire for shelter in his limbs, but above the fear settled the same curiosity that had kept him in the square.

At last, Nemi peered out from the blankets with one eye.

“The ceiling is very far away,” she whispered.

“That’s what I told your father.”

“It might fall.”

“Then we’ll have time to see it coming.”

Sava heard and looked up from the data. To Taren, the sentence was reassuring. To her, it described every catastrophe.

As they entered Vaor, the train slowed. Scratch marks appeared on the tunnel walls, sparse at first, then so dense that the rock looked combed by claws. Oru brought his face close to the window.

“Those aren’t thermal cracks.”

“Root consumers?” Sava asked.

“Too high. And too large.”

The train stopped before the station.

The lights went out for a moment, then returned on backup power. Three faint vibrations rose from the floor. The travellers folded their sails, and some were already standing to move towards the exits.

Taren laid his palm down.

“It isn’t an alarm,” he said. “The intervals are equal.”

“Perhaps the local system uses a different code,” Sava said.

“Their bodies wouldn’t use a different code.”

The door between the carriages opened. An official of House Veyr entered, breathing hard.

“A herd has broken the main pipe. We’ll have to walk into the city.”

“What herd?” Oru asked.

The official avoided answering.

They got off into a service tunnel. The floor was covered with pieces of shattered ceramic root and a nutrient liquid that smelled of fermented sugar. Two hundred metres ahead, the pipe had been split open like a broken bone. Around it moved dozens of grey animals, low and broad, with translucent plates on their backs. Some were Nemi’s size, others almost as big as an adult. They drove their jaws into the pipe’s tissue and tore out the living fibres.

“Glassbacks,” Oru said. “I thought they only remained in the western reserve.”

“The reserve hasn’t had a fence for seven years,” the official replied.

“Why haven’t you driven them away with ultrasound?”

“They’ve got used to it.”

“Chemical repellents?”

“They eat them.”

One animal lifted its snout. Its small eyes expressed no fear, because it had evolved after the Cleansing, in a world with nothing to hunt it. It approached the group and bit a traveller’s bag.

Taren pushed it away with his foot. The glassback came straight back.

“The biological protection law allows lethal removal when vital infrastructure is threatened,” Sava said.

The official lowered his voice.

“Only with approval from Ivarra. The latest request is nine months old.”

Taren looked at the pipe. Nutrient spilled into the tunnel and vanished through the grilles. In the city, every litre would be deducted from rations.

“Do you have cutting tools?”

“For roots, not animals.”

“Any tool cuts if you don’t ask what’s written on it.”

Sava caught his arm.

“You have no authority.”

“I’m the security officer. The position was invented yesterday. Its limits haven’t been set yet.”

Daran Veyr appeared from the back of the column. His provincial cloak was drawn tightly around his body, and he watched Taren with interest.

“The limits are usually set after the first act,” he said.

Taren took a bladed hook from a technician, a tool used to strip diseased tissue from pipes. He approached the animal that had attacked the bag. The glassback did not run. Taren set the blade beneath its neck plate and pulled.

The death was less dramatic than the one in the square. The animal contracted, made a wet sound and fell. The others kept eating.

A few travellers turned their heads. A mother covered her child’s eyes. Daran did not move.

“One changes nothing,” Taren said.

“You’ve just broken a planetary law,” Sava replied.

“Then the law should come and repair the pipe.”

Twelve young people appeared from the side gallery. They wore no House insignia, and their clothes were sewn from scraps of agricultural fabric. They moved with their backs close to the walls, but none recoiled at the sight of blood.

The one in front was a short female with an old notch in her right sail.

“Will you give us tools?” she asked.

Daran raised an eyebrow.

“Who are you?”

“The ones who eat from this pipe when there’s nothing left in the depot.”

“Your names.”

“We have none in the registers.”

Vessa Dahr took each of them in with a single glance. She recognised Sava by the Chamber badge, Oru by his green nails, Nemi by the splint on her sail. With Taren, she paused at the bandage on his shoulder and the way he held the dirty hook.

“The others are waiting for you to tell them what to do,” she said. “We have no time left to wait.”

She slipped through the false panel. Beyond it there was no station lighting, but rows of small lamps connected directly to the pipes. The air was warmer and damper. It smelled of bodies, milk, mould and soup thinned too many times.

The sound of children came before the sight of them.

Sava stopped on the threshold. Her map had shown empty agricultural chambers. Ahead, a little girl was falling asleep in a harvest basket, two adolescents shared a bowl, and beneath every root an inhabited niche opened. It was not a temporary camp. It was a city that had learned to leave no administrative traces.

Vessa took off her stained gloves.

“Welcome to the population that was not supposed to exist,” she said.


CHAPTER VII

THE SECOND VAOR

TAREN · VAOR · THE FIRST WEEK

The “second Vaor” appeared on no map. Yet it had midwives, markets, rules of neighbourliness, debts, songs and dead — all the things through which a crowd becomes a city before the state grants it a name.

The young woman’s name was Vessa Dahr, and she was a doctor only because no one else had agreed to care for the hidden settlements. She led them through a gallery concealed by insulation panels. Beyond it, beneath the agricultural chambers declared abandoned, stretched a second Vaor.

There were no squares, schools or separate dwellings there. Families slept in niches dug between the supply roots. Children ran naked through the warm steam, so many that the floor vibrated continuously beneath their footsteps. Sava reflexively counted the first twenty small bodies, then gave up. Every adult carried the scent of more relatives than any legal file allowed.

“How many?” she asked.

Vessa pulled off her gloves, stained with the animal’s blood.

“Here, eleven thousand. In all the chambers, perhaps eighty thousand.”

“The model indicates more.”

“The model includes glassbacks. They like the nutrient mixture for newborns.”

“Did Daran know?”

“Daran knows as much as it is useful for him to know.”

The lord of Vaor had entered the chamber behind them. He did not deny it.

“Without these children,” he said, “the domain’s population would have fallen below the maintenance threshold within two generations. The capital grants us births according to reported yield. Yield falls because the mirror system favours the central districts. With less food we get fewer births; with fewer births we have fewer hands; with fewer hands we produce still less. Your Measure kills us without spilling blood.”

“You concealed eighty thousand people,” Sava said.

“I refused to declare them impossible.”

“And now you can’t feed them.”

“That is why I requested the right to defend our harvests.”

Sava looked at the children. Most had unusually mobile auditory sails and started at every noise. A few, however, watched Taren without blinking. A very small girl approached and touched the bandage on his shoulder.

“Are you the one who killed the shadow?” she asked.

The news had travelled faster than the train.

The girl who had asked Taren about the shadow was called Eri, and she was not seven or eight, as he had assumed. She was fourteen. Malnutrition had kept her body small, and an early pregnancy was hidden beneath a loose tunic. When Vessa asked her to take the dressings to the clinic, Eri stood carefully, bracing one hand against the wall.

Sava noticed the movement.

“Is she pregnant?”

“Not in the registers,” Vessa said.

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“Then you know the answer.”

The clinic occupied a former germination chamber. The beds were lowered shelves, separated by packing cloth. In one corner, three newborns slept in a transport tray warmed by a pipe. None wore an identity bracelet.

Sava opened her terminal.

“I can issue temporary status. Without it, they can’t receive medical rations, vaccinations or places on a train.”

An old woman immediately drew the nearest child to her chest.

“And once you give them a number, where will you move them?”

“I’m not the one who moves them.”

“Your number makes them movable.”

Conversations stopped around Sava. To the people below, the Chamber badge meant neither food nor recognition, but a door that could open onto expulsion. She realised she had come prepared to find fraud, and that to those before her, her very existence was the threat.

She closed the terminal.

“Then I’ll start without names,” she said. “We count pipes, beds and portions. The identities stay with you until we establish what protection they can receive.”

“And if you can’t establish it?”

“Then at least I’ll know exactly how large the lie is that we’re leaving them in.”

Vessa looked at her with less hostility, but no more warmth.

“Here, accuracy is an expensive form of compassion.”

• • •

Oru spent the first day in the galleries. Vaor’s root tissue was old, repaired dozens of times with incompatible organisms. In some places, fibres had grown through metal and used it as a skeleton; in others, maintenance bacteria had formed white crusts that strangled the flow. Workers marked problems with knots of string because the official sensors had been disconnected to conceal consumption.

A technician named Bes showed him a jammed valve.

“If we register it, Ivarra sees the flow,” he said. “If we don’t, we don’t get the part.”

“How do you repair it?”

“We take the part from another pipe and hope that one doesn’t break in the same cycle.”

“That isn’t repair.”

“It’s Vaor.”

Oru took off his cloak, pushed half his body into the opening and began clearing the deposits. After an hour, his fur was matted with nutrient and his face black with mineral. Bes watched him in amazement.

“Engineers from the capital send drones.”

“Drones can’t smell what’s gone wrong.”

“Neither will you after another hour.”

Oru laughed. It was the first time since the Festival that Sava had heard no worry in his laughter. Work made him whole. In Ivarra, the network was so well maintained that his profession had become the interpretation of models. Here, every decision flowed immediately through a pipe and reached someone’s bowl.

Sava found him in the evening, eating beside the workers. She held out a towel.

“You missed the meeting with Daran.”

“The pipe couldn’t be deferred.”

“Daran can.”

“Then why do you look tired?”

She sat down. Around them, people ate a paste of fermented root, sourer than the capital’s food. No one spoke of the dead in the square or the mission. They discussed a filter, a couple who had separated, a child who had stolen salt. Life continued not through solemnity, but through small problems that refused to wait for the great answers.

“They don’t want to be registered,” Sava said.

“I understand them.”

“Without registers I can’t demonstrate the need.”

“You can demonstrate consumption.”

“The centre will call it inefficiency.”

Oru broke off a piece of mycelium bread and gave it to her.

“Then show it how inefficiency lives.”

The workers’ meal continued long after the bowls were empty. Not because they had extra food, but because each vessel had to be rinsed in the same water, which was then poured into a crop tank. In Ivarra, cleaning made things disappear. Here, nothing disappeared; it simply passed into another use.

Bes led them into a side gallery where his family shared three niches with two other families. There was no door. A fibre curtain separated the space from passers-by, and plaques bearing names, debts and care shifts hung on the wall. Sava counted without meaning to: seven adults, twelve children, two visible pregnancies and an old man whose breathing had a wet wheeze.

“Don’t bother,” Bes told her. “The number will change by morning.”

“Is someone being born?”

“Moving in. Or making up. Sometimes it’s the same thing.”

The children ate first, from a shared vessel. The smallest had settled in the centre, where the adults’ bodies protected them from the draught. No one had asked them to. The group’s shape closed around them whenever someone passed quickly through the gallery. Sava noticed the movement and thought of the Chamber’s simulations, in which a family was a set of individual consumers linked by degrees of kinship. No model represented the way one sail raised in alarm raised all the others before the danger was known.

Nemi sat beside Kol, the boy from the train. He had arrived with the group from Vel Four and had been assigned temporarily to Bes’s family because his mother would be working the night shift. The two were trying to break apart a piece of hardened bread without crumbling it. A smaller girl watched them and, each time they managed to break off a corner, took it and ran.

“She’s stealing,” Nemi said.

“She’s four,” Kol replied.

“She’s still stealing.”

“At four, if you can take something from beside two older children, you deserve it.”

Sava waited for Bes to correct him. He was pouring soup for the old man and did not seem to have heard. At last, Nemi broke off a piece and deliberately placed it close to the edge. The girl took it, but this time stopped a few steps away and brought them a lump of grey salt in return.

“Now it isn’t stealing,” Kol said.

An argument broke out at the end of the gallery. Two women were tugging at the same small sack of salt, and a circle had formed around them. They were not shouting. Their voices stayed low, but their feet struck the floor in short rhythms, carrying anger through the whole root. The children stopped eating. The adults’ sails rose at once.

“Salt from the depot,” Bes explained. “Each niche receives an amount according to its numbers. One says the other has listed her dead child as alive.”

Sava stood.

“I can check the rations.”

Bes touched her arm.

“That’s exactly why you shouldn’t be the first to step in.”

The old man with the laboured breathing emerged from the crowd. He sat on the floor between the women and struck once with his heel. Everyone fell silent. He was not the formal leader; Sava learned later that he had been chosen only for this dispute, because he was related to neither woman and could no longer consume salt for medical reasons.

Each woman gave her version. The first, Nea, had raised her sister’s child after her sister’s death and counted him in her own niche. The second, Lemi, maintained that the same child also appeared in his father’s niche, producing two portions. Nea admitted it. The father had been away on outside work for six days, and his portion would have been withdrawn if the family reported his absence. They had used it to feed a sick newborn.

“So she lied,” Lemi said.

“Yes,” the old man replied.

“Then the salt is ours.”

“Not all of it.”

He divided the sack in three. One share went to Lemi’s family, one to Nea’s, and the third to the clinic caring for the newborn. Nea had to take on two condensation shifts for the wronged niche. The absent father would remain registered until his return, but his portion was publicly marked. If he came back and demanded it, the whole gallery would know which child would be left without food.

“You’ve legalised fraud,” Sava said after the circle dispersed.

“We’ve made it visible,” Bes replied.

“And if everyone lies for a real need?”

“Then we learn that the rule produces more needs than it can bear.”

“Or that there aren’t enough resources.”

“We know that already. We don’t need the capital to explain hunger to us.”

Sava looked at the plaques on the wall. They were not registers in the Chamber’s sense. Some names were scratched out, others moved between niches, others accompanied by signs she could not read. Yet the local system kept a precise memory of obligations. The difference was not between order and chaos, but between an order that accepted the exception in plain sight and one that hid the exception in the data to preserve its elegance.

Later, the children began a game. One struck the floor in a code, and the others had to reach the right niche without speaking. Nemi thought it was a competition and ran. Kol caught her cloak.

“The first one doesn’t win.”

“Then who does?”

“The group, if no one is missing.”

The codes represented accidents: smoke in the upper gallery, a collapse, contaminated water, a blocked entrance. The older children carried the little ones. Those with poor hearing kept a hand on the wall. In each round, someone deliberately stayed behind, and the group had to notice. There was no prize. At the end, an adolescent counted breaths and sent them back if they were not all there.

“Do they do this for fun?” Sava asked Eri.

“We do it because two years ago a pipe caught fire and seven children ran the wrong way.”

“Did they die?”

“Three did. Afterwards, the little ones started being afraid of the drill. Vessa said we should turn it into a game.”

Nemi came back sweating, holding the hand of the little girl who had stolen the bread. In the last round, Nemi had been chosen to stay behind. Kol had found her by the mineral smell of the splint on her sail.

“We don’t have this game in Ivarra,” Nemi said.

“Don’t pipes burn in Ivarra?” Eri asked.

“They do,” Sava replied. “But we have doors that close themselves.”

“Then your doors play your game.”

Before sleep, a young woman began a song, and the others answered with gentle blows on the floor. The verses listed the names of galleries, springs and places where the root was thick enough to withstand a collapse. It sounded like a lullaby. It was also an evacuation map. At the refrain, the children named those who had to be looked for first: the little ones, the sick, those without hearing, then anyone who did not answer.

Sava listened and wondered how many of Vaor’s practices had been called primitive only because they did not fit a central protocol. Then she looked at Eri, pregnant at fourteen, at the newborn fed through a lie and the old man breathing without medicine. Their community was not a pure alternative to the Chamber. It was an intelligence built under pressure, capable of turning deprivation into solidarity and solidarity into obligation. It protected those it could. It put the others to use early.

Nea held out a blanket to Sava.

“Sleep beside the wall. If the smoke signal comes, the children go over you.”

“Is there room for everyone?”

Nea looked at the already full niche.

“No. But we all sleep.”

That night, Sava slept in Eri’s family niche. It was wide enough for four, but nine people lived there. The little ones lay in the centre, the adults at the edges. When someone turned, everyone else had to move. Sava felt every breath through the floor and understood why the people below did not react to crowding as those in the capital did. They had not been selected for quiet. They had been forced to invent it.

At mid-cycle, Eri woke and went out. Sava followed her to a condensation basin.

“Vessa says you’re not allowed to carry water,” she said.

“Vessa says a lot of things when she isn’t the one who has to do them.”

Sava took the container.

“Who is the father?”

Eri raised one sail.

“Why? So you can fill in the field?”

“So I know whether he’s here.”

“He is. He won’t be given a name either.”

“A child without an identity can’t travel, can’t inherit space, can’t enter an official school.”

“A child with an identity can be taken away.”

“We don’t take children.”

“You move them with their parents to places where they die more slowly and call it redistribution.”

Sava held the container beneath the drip. Each drop sounded too loud.

“I can’t change anything if I don’t know who you are.”

“Perhaps the problem is that you only change what you can count.”

Eri took the water and left. Sava remained beside the basin until the pipe stopped.

• • •

Taren spent the same night with the children in the lower chamber. He had not meant to. A little girl had asked him to repeat how he had killed the saryx, and the story had drawn other children. In their version, the creature grew larger each time. By the fourth telling, it had eyes of fire and had swallowed half the square.

“That’s not how it happened,” Taren said.

“Then tell it better,” a boy replied.

He showed them the rod, the position of the mouth, the way the animal followed vibrations. The children did not want metaphors. They wanted details they could use. How had he held his hands? Where had it hurt? Had he been afraid? Why had he not run?

“I was afraid,” he said. “It just didn’t tell me what to do straight away.”

“It tells us,” Eri said from the doorway.

Taren looked at her. No one had told him about her pregnancy, but now he saw it.

“And do you listen?”

“Sometimes. Other times I have nowhere to go.”

The sentence followed him as Vessa led him towards the dead man’s room.

“How?”

Taren sat at her level.

“I stayed where I was until I saw what could be done.”

“Mother says to run before I see.”

“Your mother wants you to live.”

“Don’t you?”

Taren hesitated.

“Not only that.”

Vessa heard his answer. She had already examined him with a doctor’s eye: the position of his shoulders, the lack of residual trembling, the way his attention did not jump towards every exit.

“You’ve been treated for delayed dispersal,” she said.

“Eleven years.”

“With synthetic veyrin?”

“Until I was fourteen.”

“Why did they stop?”

“I started biting the therapists.”

Vessa showed her chisel teeth in a quick smile.

“Good biological response.”

Sava came closer.

“It’s a dysfunction, not a virtue.”

“In Ivarra, perhaps. Here, half the accidents happen because workers abandon their tools at the first signal and block the tunnels. When the gangs from the Night turn up, the guards run away along with the thieves.”

“What gangs?”

Daran folded his cloak over his arms.

“Nivari without a House. Unregistered children from other domains, expelled when they grew up. They steal food, batteries, medicine. They don't usually kill.”

“Usually?”

“Until last month, never.”

Vessa led them into a side room. A worker's body lay on a low platform, covered to the chest. His throat had been cut with a narrow blade. There were no defensive wounds on his hands.

“He froze,” she said. “They asked him for the depot code. He stood still and hoped they would leave. A good reflex against a hunter looking for movement. Less good against someone who knows what a code is.”

A simple shape had been drawn in blood on the wall above the corpse: an oval and two lines pointing forwards, like a face seen from the front. Faces were almost absent from nivar art. Portraits were made in profile, to keep the eyes in their natural position. There was something obscene about the image.

“What does it mean?” Sava asked.

“That they didn't turn to run,” Vessa replied.

In the frontal drawing, the eyes were two short lines directed at the viewer. For a nivar, a face seen this way could not properly encompass the field of vision. It had to relinquish half the world to give the other person its full attention. That was why the image suggested not only courage but a threat: *I see you, and I accept not seeing what comes from the side*.

Taren touched the dried blood on the wall.

“The thieves drew this?”

“Or someone wants us to think so,” Vessa said.

“Daran?”

“Daran prefers symbols he can control.”

“Seln?”

“Seln prefers not to leave blood on walls.”

Vessa covered the dead man. His name was Deh, he was twenty-two, and he had three younger brothers. He had guarded the depot because the job came with an extra ration. He had not been a soldier. There were no soldiers.

A series of knocks came from the main gallery: one, a pause, two close together.

Vessa's sails rose abruptly.

“The northern depot.”

On their way out, they found the young people who had killed the animals gathered around Taren. Some still carried their dirty hooks. When the signal repeated, almost all of them took a step back.

Taren thought of Eri: sometimes fear tells you what to do; sometimes there is nowhere to go.

He took a step forwards.

Seven followed him.


CHAPTER VIII

THE NORTHERN DEPOT

TAREN · VAOR · THE EIGHTH DAY

At the northern depot, peace did not end with a declaration. It ended when a group of young people entered a corridor that everyone else was leaving.

That same evening, Taren gathered the twelve young people beside the broken pipe. Thirty more joined them, then seventy. Daran had provided hooks, nets and masks. Sava drafted a provisional authorisation for lethal removal, limited to organisms affecting the infrastructure. It was legal only if someone accepted a generous interpretation of the word provisional.

Taren showed them how to move without striking the floor. How to lure the animals with vibrations produced at a distance. How not to leap back when the jaws snapped shut. He had learned these things nowhere. He worked them out as he explained them.

Some of the young people could not do it. At the first attack, they broke formation and retreated. Taren did not scold them. He moved them to the nets, to carrying, to cleaning the pipes. Those who stayed beside him acquired a new kind of silence.

By the change in orbital light, they had killed a hundred and nineteen animals. The meat, declared unfit for consumption under central law, was taken to the hidden chambers. For the first time in many days, the children ate until their bellies rounded.

Vessa had stitched a new cut on Taren's forearm. They sat together beside the tunnel entrance, watching the workers repair the pipe.

“In the old populations,” she said, “one in several thousand was born like you. Most disappeared before they reproduced. The predators did the selecting for us.”

“Now the Chamber does it.”

“The Chamber is gentler. It lets you live. It just makes sure there don't become too many of you.”

Taren looked at the children sharing the meat.

“Here, no one made sure.”

“Exactly.”

“How many are like me?”

Vessa turned one sail towards the group of young people. Seven still stood beside the carcasses without avoiding the smell.

“Enough for a team,” she said. “Not enough for a world.”

Three knocks sounded from deep in the tunnel.

This time the intervals were uneven. The first came alone. The other two, close together.

Vessa stood up.

“That's the northern depot code.”

A young man came running, a cut on his cheek.

“The people from the Night have come. Twenty, maybe more. They have thermal projectiles.”

Everyone around them took a step back.

Taren took one forwards.

Seven of them followed him.

Leth was the last of the seven. Taren could hear his breathing a few steps behind, too fast for the short run. The young man was sixteen, with thin arms and a left sail that had not fully risen since a childhood infection. During the killing of the animals, he had worked at the nets, far from the jaws, but he had come now because his brother was guarding the depot.

“You can stay at the junction,” Taren told him.

“No.”

“It isn't a test.”

“I know.”

“Then why are you shaking?”

Leth looked at him with one eye.

“Because I don't want to die.”

It was a better answer than all the declarations Taren had heard since the square.

“Stay behind Vessa. When she says down, you crouch. When she says out, you leave. Don't interpret.”

“And you?”

“I interpret.”

They reached the depot at the same time as the second projectile. The heat melted an edge of the ceiling, and the smell of burnt stone filled the gallery.

The northern depot had been designed for petty theft, not defence. Its doors opened wide, its corridors were straight, and its alarm system transmitted vibration in every direction. By the time Taren arrived, the local guards had already crowded into the evacuation gallery, each trying not to be last.

The first thermal projectile struck the ceiling a pace from him. The rock reddened and swelled, then burst into molten drops. The seven who had followed him threw themselves to the ground. Taren remained standing a fraction longer, long enough to see where the light had come from.

“Turn everything off,” he said.

“They see heat,” Vessa replied. “Darkness won't help us.”

“Not for them.”

He tore the alarm panel off the wall and broke the transmission fibre. The floor stopped pulsing. In the sudden silence, the attackers could no longer count the fugitives by their footsteps, and the defenders no longer received the biological command to scatter.

“Cover the hot pipes with wet sacks,” Taren continued. “Open the cold rooms. I want steam.”

No one asked him for authorisation. Sometimes a position became real only because the others had no time to invent a better one.

The young people moved. Vessa pulled two guards back from the evacuation gallery and sent them to the valves. Cold air flooded the depot, met the warm nutrient leak, and filled the corridors with dense mist. To the projectiles' thermal senses, every cloud became a target.

“How many?” Taren asked.

A guard pressed his jaw to the floor, trying to distinguish the vibrations.

“Maybe twenty. Maybe thirty.”

“Stop listening to the floor. They know you're listening to it too.”

To demonstrate, Taren threw a metal rod into the corridor on the left. The attackers fired at once. Three thermal needles passed through the mist and struck the empty wall.

“Listen to the air,” he said.

The sails around him rose. Amid the hissing of the valves and the dripping pipes, they could hear controlled breathing, the rustle of clothes, one chisel tooth involuntarily rubbing against another.

Taren divided the group in two. He had no military training; no one did. He used instincts that culture had kept separate: the silence of the hunted, the approach from the side, the violent leap of the hind limbs, the ability to change direction without warning. The only novelty was that none of these served retreat any longer.

They found the first three attackers beside the seed chamber. They wore black ceramic masks with the frontal shape drawn in white. One held a homemade thermal projector; the others were loading sacks.

Taren raised his hand. His group stopped.

He could have let the thieves leave. The food in the room would have kept the hidden settlements for two days. A well-aimed projectile could kill. Behind him, one of the young men, Leth, was shaking so hard that the rod he held struck the floor rhythmically.

The armed attacker turned his mask towards the sound.

Taren leapt.

His hind limbs propelled him through the mist. He struck the attacker's wrist before the needle discharged. The thermal light passed beside Vessa and charred the sacks against the wall. The other two retreated, not towards the exit but into opposite corners, forcing them to divide their attention.

Taren seized the projector. The attacker did not let go. They looked at each other in profile, each with one eye on the other and one on the corridor. Then the black mask came closer until their foreheads collided.

“You're the one from the capital,” the stranger said.

“You're the one who cut the worker's throat.”

“He had the code.”

“You could have left him alive.”

“He could have given it to us.”

The stranger thrust the weapon between them. Taren caught his forearm and felt a thin, bony body beneath the clothes. He was not fighting a warrior, but someone who had eaten too little for too long.

To the right, Vessa struck a second attacker with a hook. Leth ran. The third thief fired after him. The thermal needle entered Leth between the shoulder blades and came out through his chest in a white cloud.

Leth's death stopped nothing. His body fell, the next projectile came, and Vessa had to step around him to reach the attacker on the right. Taren saw the young man's face for only a fraction of a moment: the quiet surprise of someone who had thought death would have time to announce itself.

A rule broke inside him.

Taren felt something inside him break, not like pain, but like a rule.

He struck the stranger with his forehead. The mask cracked. The projector fell. When the stranger tried to retreat, Taren followed. He struck him in the knee, then in the throat, then once more after he had fallen.

Vessa caught his shoulder.

“Enough.”

Taren turned towards her with his mouth open. His front chisel teeth were a few centimetres from her throat. Vessa did not move.

It was not an absence of fear that held her there. Taren could smell it clearly. Fear came through her skin, bitter and sharp. But she had chosen an action different from the one her body demanded.

Taren closed his mouth.

The attacker on the floor tore off his mask. He was young, perhaps Taren's age, with a circular scar around his muzzle.

“Raal,” Vessa said. “I thought you were dead.”

“I am, in the registers.”

“Who gave you the projectiles?”

Raal spat blood.

“Those who have enough.”

Footsteps came from the neighbouring corridors. The rest of the gang were approaching, but not in an attack formation. They called the names of the three. Children could be heard among the voices.

Taren looked at Leth's body. Smoke still rose from the wound. The young man had run and died precisely because he had run in a straight line.

“How much food do you want?” Taren asked.

Raal laughed and groaned at the same time.

“All of it.”

“How much do you need to leave?”

“We aren't going anywhere. The Night pushes us back, the Day burns us, the domains remove us from the registers. This is the only place with food.”

“And the only place you rob.”

“Because it's the only place that gave birth to us.”

When the rest of the gang appeared, Taren still held Raal's arm twisted behind his back. The attackers did not advance. There were fourteen adults and six children, all thin, dressed in layers patched with thermal insulation. One boy's toes were blackened by frostbite. A woman wore two empty batteries round her neck like ornaments.

“Put down your weapons,” Taren said.

“Let Raal go,” the woman replied.

“Weapons first.”

The children pressed against the wall. One was crying, but without a sound. Vessa moved slowly between the two groups and knelt beside Leth. She put her fingers to his throat, although the hole in his chest made the gesture pointless. Then she covered the body with her own cloak.

“One of ours is dead,” said a young man behind Taren. “We're not giving them food.”

“Whose was he?” Raal asked from the floor. “Yesterday he didn't have a proper number. Today he's yours because he died with you?”

The young man raised his hook. Taren gestured for him to lower it.

“How long can you last in the Night?” he asked Raal.

“Without batteries, two cycles. The children, one.”

“Why don't you come to the hidden chambers?”

“Because Daran takes every body he sees and puts it to work for a House's debt. We have no House.”

“But you were born here.”

“That's precisely why we know what his promises are worth.”

Taren released the pressure on his arm. Raal struggled to his feet. The circular scar around his muzzle was the mark of a thermal mask worn too long.

“The projectiles,” Taren said. “All of them. You get food for three days and a charged battery. After that, come without weapons and we'll talk.”

“Talk about what?”

“How you stop killing people for codes.”

“And you stop removing us from the world?”

“I can't promise on behalf of the world.”

Raal bared his chisel teeth, not in a smile.

“Then promise on behalf of your blade. It's the only institution that arrived in time.”

Daran Veyr arrived with forty House agents after the fighting was over. The steam had dispersed, and the surviving thieves sat disarmed in the seed chamber. Taren had given them twenty sacks and asked for the thermal projectiles in return. Raal had agreed only after Vessa had bound his broken arm.

“You paid them for stealing,” Daran said.

“I bought the weapons.”

“With my food.”

“With the food of the children you don't officially acknowledge.”

Daran looked at Leth's body, then at the seven young people remaining beside Taren. Five had blood on their clothes. Two had drawn on their chests, with a finger, the frontal sign found on the dead man's wall. Not in white, like the thieves, but in Leth's red.

“You lost one and gained a symbol,” Daran said. “A favourable exchange by the rules of history.”

Taren caught the edge of his cloak and pulled him close.

All the House agents took a step, but Daran raised his hand.

“You knew they would come,” Taren said.

“I knew lack of food would bring them, sooner or later.”

“You left the depot with guards who couldn't fight.”

“None of our guards can fight. That's why I brought you.”

“You didn't bring me.”

“Do you think Seln Aru sends questions without someone having written to him first?”

Taren let him go. Daran smoothed his cloak.

“Vaor needed a fact the capital could not postpone,” he continued. “Now we have a death, clandestine weapons and a gang controlling the frontier galleries. Tomorrow I will ask again for the right to a guard.”

“Leth didn't die for your petition.”

“The dead don't keep their reasons. The living use them.”

Sava arrived before Taren could hit him. Without thinking, she placed herself between them, her body turned face-on to her brother. She saw him notice the gesture.

“Daran,” she said, “any petition will include the fact that you concealed the population and left the infrastructure unprotected.”

“Include the fact that a Chamber official authorised the killing of a hundred and nineteen protected organisms.”

“I saved a vital pipe.”

“And I'm trying to save a vital domain.”

“By provoking a crisis.”

“The crisis existed. I merely gave it a room with light.”

After Daran left, the depot smelled of steam, blood and charred seeds. Taren helped lift Leth. The young man's brother had arrived by then; he was the guard who had fled first into the evacuation gallery. When he saw the body, he sat on the floor without a sound.

“He told me to stay behind,” he said. “I told him he was a child.”

Taren did not know whether he was speaking to him.

“He came for you.”

The guard looked at him.

“Then you let him.”

Vessa stopped cleaning her instruments. Taren felt all the available answers: Leth had chosen; no one had been forced; the attack would have killed more without them. All were true. None could be placed beside the body without becoming obscene.

“Yes,” he said.

The guard struck his forehead against the wall once. Vessa caught him before the second blow.

The following night, at the simple ceremony in the hidden chambers, Leth's mother asked that the frontal sign not be drawn on the dead man.

“He didn't have the sign when he left home,” she said. “Don't put it on him after he can no longer say no.”

Daran had sent a brown House cloth. The woman refused it. Sava had offered her an official posthumous registration, which would give the family a compensatory ration. This she accepted.

“Isn't it the same thing?” Nemi asked after the ceremony. “You're still putting something on him after he's died.”

Sava straightened her cloak.

“The ration helps his brothers.”

“And the sign helps the others be brave.”

“That's precisely why it's more dangerous.”

Nemi looked towards Taren. He stood alone beside the water pipe, his hands washed and blood still caught beneath his chisel teeth.

“Is he dangerous?”

Sava did not answer at once.

“Yes,” she said at last. “And sometimes his danger helps us. That's the part you have to remember whole.”

• • •

Over the following days, Daran turned the death into a file, Sava into a procedural violation, and the lower chambers into a song. Taren tried to preserve the order of events. He repeated that Leth had run. He repeated that Raal had not been defeated, but bought. He repeated that the attackers had been hungry.

No one really listened. People were not looking for accuracy; they were looking for a form in which a young man's death could be something more than loss.

On the fifth day, Raal returned alone and unarmed. He put the last battery on the table, although it was still half charged.

“You said we'd talk.”

“About codes,” Taren said.

“Codes are what people who have food talk about. I want to talk about who guards the door after you open it.”

“You want to join the unit.”

“I want to see whether the ones with blades still eat first when there's no more fighting.”

Taren pointed to the training circle.

“Then you start at the bottom.”

Raal raised one sail towards the suspended trunk.

“Everyone who claims not to have an army starts with an exercise,” he said.


CHAPTER IX

THE FIRST FACE

SAVA, TAREN AND VESSA · VAOR · THE FIRST TWO MONTHS

The First Face began as a temporary authorisation to protect agriculture. It became an institution when the inhabitants began to imagine their future through it.

In the days that followed, the story changed every time it was told. At first, seven young people had defended the depot. Then there had been five. Then three. In the version sung in the hidden chambers, Taren had fought forty thieves alone, and Leth had died without taking a step back.

Taren corrected every version until he understood that truth was not competing with lies. It was competing with need.

Daran received approval for a “temporary agricultural protection unit”, limited to a hundred members and without the right to thermal weapons. That same day, three hundred young people presented themselves at the depot. Most were unregistered. Some had come for food. Others because they had heard that unit members would get rooms of their own. Many watched Taren with the kind of attention he had received only once before, after the saryx's death.

Vessa examined them. She measured their reaction time, veyrin secretion, hind-limb density and ability to remain functional under alarm vibrations. Sava found her in a room full of young people waiting to have blood taken.

“You're creating a biological register for combat,” Sava said.

“I'm creating a medical register.”

“You're selecting them for lack of fear.”

“No. Half the good ones are very frightened. I select them for what they do while they're afraid.”

“It's the same distinction the Chamber uses when it claims it doesn't prohibit births.”

Vessa closed the test tube.

“Then perhaps I learned from the best.”

A genetic model hung on her wall. Several lines intersected around a group of neural markers.

“Taren's trait isn't a single thing,” she continued. “The delayed response comes from seven known variations, and probably others. In the registered population, the combination appears in one in nine thousand. Here, among those born without filtering, it's one in seven hundred.”

“Because of inbreeding?”

“Partly. But mostly because families hide those whom the capital would treat or sterilise.”

“We don't sterilise.”

Vessa looked at her face-on.

“What do you call a permit postponed beyond reproductive age?”

Sava felt her own file like an invisible wound.

In the courtyard of House Veyr, Taren was teaching recruits to stand before a suspended weight. A root trunk swung towards their chests, and they had to evade it at the last moment, not by fleeing but with a short turn that preserved their position. Those who retreated too early left the circle. Those who waited too long were struck.

Taren did not know how to teach. At first, he demonstrated everything once and assumed the others had seen what he had seen. Raal made him repeat it. Vessa explained why a recruit was shaking. Sava asked him to write rules before every improvisation became a tradition.

The three were neither subordinates nor friends. That was precisely why the unit did not yet speak with a single voice.

In the morning circle, the recruits assembled in groups of twelve. Some came from the unregistered chambers, where they had learned to sleep amid noise and share every object. Others were legal children of the House, accustomed to regular portions and the idea that authority smelled of their own family. The first despised discipline; the others despised dirt. Taren mixed them deliberately.

One day an administrator's son, Emon Veyr, refused to clean the post's latrine.

“I came to guard pipes,” he said.

“A latrine is a pipe,” replied Oru, who was there for a repair.

The recruits laughed. Emon flushed beneath his fur.

“My father runs the Warm district.”

Raal held out the brush.

“Then you have family experience of what piles up when no one cleans.”

Taren stopped Raal before the joke became a fight, but put Emon on cleaning duty for three cycles. On the fourth day, Emon worked without being called. Later, he would command a Vertical House and claim his discipline had been born there. No song would mention the brush.

The first exercise outside the courtyard was scheduled in a disused irrigation gallery. Daran wanted spectators. Taren refused them. He said a unit that learned to be watched before it learned to make mistakes would hide every mistake. Daran agreed and sent only two House men, three scribes and an image operator.

“These are spectators,” Taren said.

“These are administrative continuity,” the operator replied.

Raal put him out the door. The scribes stayed because Sava insisted that the exercise have an independent report.

The gallery had been chosen for its three exits, an old pump and enough space for the groups to lose one another without getting far apart. Oru had checked the structure that morning. He marked a crack on the wall and asked Taren not to let more than forty people into the central chamber.

“I have a hundred,” Taren said.

“Then rotate them.”

“They need to learn to move together.”

“The root isn't impressed by your educational needs.”

Taren divided them into three groups. The first had to guard the pump while Raal triggered noises and lights in the side galleries. The second carried empty containers as if they were wounded people. The third received contradictory information and had to decide what to pass on.

The rule was simple: the position had no value in itself. The pump was to be defended only while it supplied the district. If the pressure passed the threshold, it had to be abandoned and shut off from outside.

“Then why teach them to stay?” Emon Veyr asked.

“So they can leave a place because they've decided to, not because the first body in front of them ran.”

“It looks like the same movement.”

“From outside, many moral things look like muscles.”

The exercise began badly. At the first signal, two groups crowded towards the same exit. At the second, the recruits from the lower chambers completely ignored an order from a House son. At the third, a young man stood so still before a suspended weight that it struck his shoulder.

Taren stopped the mechanism.

“Why didn't you turn?”

“You said not to run.”

“I told you to preserve the function.”

“The function was the position.”

“No. The function was to stop the weight touching the pump. If it hits you and you fall onto it, you've lost both.”

The young man held his shoulder and seemed more ashamed of the correction than of the pain. The others watched him with admiration. He had been struck and had not moved. The lesson his body conveyed was simpler than Taren's explanation.

Vessa took him out of the line.

“He has a fracture,” she said.

“I can continue.”

“That's exactly why you won't.”

In the second group, Raal used an alarm device from the old depots. The sound was not loud, but it contained the low frequency that awakened the dispersal reflex. Sails rose together. Some recruits lost their central vision. Others flattened themselves against the floor. Emon started towards the exit, stopped, and returned to the container he was supposed to carry.

“You ran,” an unregistered young man told him.

“I came back.”

“In a fight, the first part is enough.”

“Then why do we practise?”

The argument did not end. The floor rose beneath them.

It was not part of the exercise. The old pump received a pressure surge from the active network, and the crack Oru had marked opened. A jet of hot nutrient struck the wall. The swollen root split along a length of three metres, and the chamber began to fill with sweetish steam.

Taren shouted the order to retreat.

Most did not move.

They thought this was the real test, hidden inside the exercise. Taren saw in their faces the determination not to be the ones who had run. His myth had already become stronger than his order.

“Get out!” he repeated.

A recruit raised the empty container as a shield and advanced towards the pump. The jet flung him back. Vessa dragged him by the leg. Raal began beating the evacuation rhythm, but the alarm drowned out the vibration.

Oru slipped towards the side console. He needed the manual key beyond the crack. Emon saw it in a bracket, a few steps from the jet. He leapt, grabbed it, and rolled to Oru. He had not held his position; he had crossed the chamber and returned. The young man from the lower chambers, the one who had accused him of fleeing, positioned himself behind him and shielded him from falling pieces of root.

Together they held the valve until Oru locked it.

The pressure fell. The steam thinned. Five wounded people lay on the floor, and a hundred people no longer knew whether they had passed the test.

Taren took them all up out of the gallery and seated them in a circle. He was furious, mostly with himself.

“I ordered a retreat.”

“We thought you were testing us,” someone said.

“I was. You failed because you preferred the story about courage to the information in front of you.”

“But we saved the pump,” Emon said.

Oru, covered in nutrient, raised the key.

“The pump was out of service. The surge came because the network was rerouted for the unit's new rooms. You risked lives to save an object that supplied no one.”

Silence settled over the circle. For the first time, the recruits looked at the pump not as the symbol of their mission but as an old component no one had checked thoroughly enough.

“Then what should we have done?” asked the young man with the injured shoulder.

“Found out what you were protecting,” Taren replied.

That same evening, he wrote the unit's first rules. Sava helped him remove words that could be interpreted as unlimited oaths. Oru removed technically impossible formulations. Vessa demanded a rule allowing the doctor to take any member off duty without the commander's approval. Raal insisted on the right to refuse an order that turned retreat into an execution.

The final document had nine lines.

*Protect the person before the sign.*

*Protect the function before the position.*

*An ordered retreat is not flight.*

*Surrender ends the fight.*

*Fear does not determine rank.*

The other four described food, weapons, reporting and responsibility for the wounded. Those would be quoted least often.

Vessa also revised the selection criteria. They rejected candidates who felt no alarm at all, because some had nerve damage and others failed to notice danger to those around them. Points went to those who could convey information while their hands shook, those who returned for a person without losing the way, and those who admitted when they had not understood an order.

“We aren't building individuals without fear,” she told Daran when he came to see the wounded. “We're building groups that don't let one person's fear become everyone's direction.”

Daran looked at the bent key, the bandaged shoulder and the crack in the wall.

“That sounds less memorable.”

“Then it's probably truer.”

By morning, the House channel announced “the Face's first trial”: a hundred young people had stayed in a collapsing gallery and saved Vaor's network. Neither the disused pump nor the ignored retreat order appeared.

Taren demanded a correction. The channel added at the end that the incident had been “also a lesson in the limits of courage”. The headline remained.

Emon received nine bonding requests that day. The young man who had stayed beneath the weight received twelve. No one sent Oru any.

Nemi watched from a platform, her splint removed and the tip of her sail still crooked. When Taren took a break, she brought him water.

“Was Leth brave too?” she asked.

Taren sat beside her.

“I don't know.”

“He ran.”

“Yes.”

“Then he wasn't.”

“Courage isn't a shape your legs make.”

“What is it?”

Taren looked at the circle. A small recruit, trembling, let the trunk pass so close it tore a few hairs from his cheek. The others shouted with joy.

“It's a story the living lay over the body's reactions,” he said. “Sometimes the story holds. Sometimes the body breaks it.”

“What story do you have?”

Before he could answer, the courtyard floor vibrated. It was not an alarm. The recruits had struck the ground once, all together, for the one who had remained in the circle.

Taren felt the pulse rising through his bones.

“I don't know yet,” he said.

But that evening, when the temporary unit's hundred members received their grey cloaks, they all stitched the sign of a face seen from the front onto their chests.

In the Chamber's register, the unit bore a twelve-word bureaucratic name.

In Vaor, it was already called the First Face.

At first, the sign of the Face was drawn with cheap pigment. Everyone made it a little differently. On some, the oval was narrow and the eyes long; on others, the shape resembled Raal's mask.
