“I'm an excellent liar.”

“That's why I keep you close.”

In the second month, Daran proposed a Festival of Staying. He wanted enormous cloths, fires on the surface and the dead people's names projected onto rock. Sava showed him the calculation: the ceremony's materials could repair two reservoirs and feed the clinics for nine days.

“People don't live on nutrient alone,” Daran said.

“They die very concretely without it.”

“They need to know what the others died for.”

“The dead no longer need your explanation.”

The ceremony took place anyway, but without fires. Workers hung a piece of burnt pipe at every entrance. Each family wrote its own name, without Daran being able to choose the order. Some pieces carried thanks to the First Face. Others accused Daran, Ivarra, the Chamber or even Taren. One carried only the words: *we don't yet know why*.

For Oru, Nemi chose a green piece cut from the pipe he had opened before dying. She wanted to write “he saved the children”. Sava stopped her.

“He did more than his last act.”

“Then what do I write?”

They stayed a long time beside the crooked root planted at the ceremony. The graft looked sick, but Bes had found a living strand and connected it to a water circuit. Around it, the soil smelled of wet minerals and ash.

Nemi wrote: *Oru Ilen. He repaired what he could touch.*

“It doesn't say he was my father.”

“Those who know you know.”

“But a long time from now?”

Sava thought of all the people reduced in archives to a function or a relationship with someone more important.

“Add that too.”

Beneath the first line, Nemi wrote: *He was my father, and not only that.*

In the fourth month, the children began playing Deaf Lake. Some were agents running across the ice, others Faces striking imaginary valves. No one wanted to be the civilian caught between them. When an instructor tried to introduce the role, the children turned it into the person who brought water after the fighting.

Taren watched once. He saw a boy imitate the way he had raised Oru's rod. The movement was wrong and heroic. He showed him the correct angle, then regretted it. The next day, all the children repeated the gesture better.

“You gave them the lesson they wanted to hear,” Vessa said.

“He could have broken his shoulder.”

“Now he'll break it precisely.”

Vessa worked in the united clinics of the upper and hidden cities. Some doctors refused patients without identities because medicines had to be accounted for. She introduced a provisional register based on biological traces rather than names. Sava approved it as a six-month measure. After six months, she extended it. After a year, the system became the basis of the new demographic administration.

In the seventh month, the first restored harvest did not cover consumption. The Civil Office reduced adult rations by eight per cent. The Faces requested an exemption for fighters. The Hands requested one for heavy labourers. The clinics requested one for pregnant women and the sick. Every request was right, and together they left only children and officials to bear the reduction.

Sava called the representatives together and placed bowls on the table containing the proposed portions for each group.

“Eat,” she said.

No one began.

“This isn't serious negotiation,” an Orm commander protested.

“It's the only serious part. The rest is titles.”

In the end, the reductions were shared, but the Faces received supplements before patrols and the workers after shifts. The difference seemed technical. By year's end, children already knew a fighter's cloak got them through the food queue faster.

In the eleventh month, Daran began the tower's foundations. Officially, it was a communications relay and a depot in case the galleries collapsed. The design included ceremonial spaces, balconies and a platform too wide for any antenna. Sava refused the materials. Daran obtained contributions from the Houses and voluntary labour from young people who wanted to wear the Face sign.

“Voluntary?” Sava asked.

“No one is obliged.”

“What do they get?”

“Priority in selection for the Corps.”

“Then the labour is the price of rank.”

“All skills have a price.”

She tried to impose wages and shift limits. The young people protested that sacrifice could not be salaried. Their parents protested that they could not eat sacrifice. Daran accepted payment and named every ration “recognition of foundation”.

The second year had no visible beginning. Nivari did not live under seasons, and Vei's light remained motionless. Time showed itself in bodies. Nemi had grown enough that Oru's cloak no longer touched the floor when she wore it. Sen had learned to stand holding Vessa's arm. Eri had given birth and returned to the clinic before Sava considered her healed. Kol, the boy from the train, was already working on filters beside Bes and sending half his ration to his family, who had stayed in Vel Four.

On the surface, the foundations finally rose beyond the edge of the rock. The first vertical plate cast a new shadow over Vaor's entrance. The inhabitants came out to look at it. Some beat the Face rhythm. Others pressed against the walls when the wind made it crack.

No one ordered the crowd to stay.

Yet most stayed until the shadow became part of the city.

The first tower rose slowly, as a building site that consumed people and materials rather than a monument. The fossilised root had to be cut into plates, heated, pressed and transported to the surface. Twelve workers died in the first year: three fell, four froze after a lift failure, five were crushed when a beam came loose. Daran called them founders. Sava entered them in the register under construction accidents.

The difference was not merely vocabulary. Founders' families received homes near the tower and additional birth rights. Accident victims' families received standard compensation. After Sava insisted, the two categories were unified. Daran accepted and renamed every accident a founding sacrifice.

“You can't win with him,” Aven Sor told her.

The former central commander now worked in the Civil Office. Ivarra had demanded his return for an inquiry; he had refused, probably knowing he would be made responsible for Deaf Lake. In Vaor, both sides distrusted him, and that was precisely why Sava considered him useful.

“I'm not trying to win,” she replied. “I'm trying to get the families food.”

“He knows. That's why he lets you correct the effect and keeps the story.”

Their office occupied a former storage hall. On one wall hung the population map, now updated with the inhabitants below. On another were the First Face's orders, the Houses' requests, energy quotas and the list of central prisoners. Every day, Sava moved bodies between categories and tried to keep the categories from becoming destinies.

Aven brought a new request. A group of sixty captive technicians wanted to stay in Vaor. Ivarra declared them illegally detained; Vaor considered them protected guests.

“Why don't they go back?” Sava asked.

“Some have families here. Others fear a trial. Seven say they get more food. Two believe in the cause.”

“Only two?”

“Causes are easier to declare when they don't include the ration.”

Sava approved individual choice and requested interviews without the Face present. The following day, Daran announced that the central specialists had freely chosen to contribute to Vaor's rebirth. It was true. It was not the whole truth.

That was how the new order was built: from correct sentences narrowing until they left no room for another interpretation.

• • •

In the first months after the lake, Taren slept in guard posts and refused House apartments. When the tower reached its fourth level, Daran offered him a room at the top.

“From there you can see every road,” he said.

“The drones see them better.”

“From there, every road can see you.”

Taren refused. Yet First Face members began climbing at night. They slept beneath the sky on platforms without edges and came down in the morning with wind-inflamed eyes. The ritual was called *the open vigil*. Taren banned it after the first fall. The young people moved it to other Houses' unfinished towers.

Vessa told him he no longer controlled what he had inspired.

“I didn't inspire the towers.”

“You made standing still admirable. The tower is your body turned into architecture.”

“Then it should have a broken shoulder.”

Vessa did not smile. She held Sen to her chest. The child was almost a year old and followed sounds with an attentive slowness. He did not startle at training blows, but cried when Vessa left the room. His biology fitted neither Daran's hopes nor Sava's simple fears.

“Sleep at home tonight,” Vessa said.

“I have an inspection in the eastern tunnel.”

“You have people for that.”

“That's precisely why I have to go. They're beginning to think rank means exemption.”

“And you're beginning to think absence from the table is a virtue.”

Taren looked at the child.

“I don't know what to do with him.”

“Hold him. Talk to him. Be here often enough that you don't become only the face on the banners.”

Sen reached towards his scar. Taren took him awkwardly. The child gripped the edge of his burned sail and pulled. Taren flinched. This time Vessa laughed.

“He has a good reflex,” she said.

Taren stayed until Sen fell asleep. At mid-cycle, a messenger came for him. A House Orm patrol had confiscated an unregistered community's food and invoked the Code. Taren left without waking Vessa.

In the morning, Sen opened his eyes and found only his father's cloak on the peg.

• • •

Sava tried to preserve for Nemi a life that was more than history being written. She enrolled her in the civil school, imposed sleeping hours and forbade her to carry the short blade Raal gave every child in his workshop. Nemi listened and found technically compliant alternatives. She attended school, then spent her evenings in the Face archive. She slept the required hours, but read in the dark with a covered slate. She did not wear the blade; she kept it in her bag.

One evening, Sava found her copying reports from Deaf Lake.

“Why these?”

“The numbers don't match.”

“Which ones?”

“Daran says three hundred and nineteen agents died. Aven's list has three hundred and eighteen and one missing technician. The technician's body appears among the dead, but his family still receives a prisoner's ration.”

Sava took the slate. Nemi was right. A small error born of chaos.

“We'll correct it.”

“The family will lose the ration.”

“They'll get death compensation.”

“Less.”

“We can't keep a dead man as alive.”

Nemi looked at her.

“Why? The Chamber kept living people as nonexistent for years.”

Sava sat beside her daughter. It was impossible to explain the ethics of registers without defending their hypocrisies.

“Because if we change the truth every time it produces a good result, soon only those who control the register will decide what is good.”

“Then his family will eat less so the register can stay pure.”

“No. We'll change the compensation.”

It took them three weeks and two arguments with Daran. The family received the same ration. Nemi learned a lesson Sava had not intended to teach: truth was necessary, but without power it remained a form of exact cruelty.

Nemi had grown quickly. At the age when Ivarra's children began studying rituals, she knew how to read military maps, recognise by smell who was lying under interrogation and repair a ventilation valve. She remembered little of her father's death. She remembered very well that Taren had taken her out of the chamber.

On the morning of the tower's inauguration, she came to her mother's office with an apprenticeship application for the Memory Corps, the archivists who accompanied the First Face and recorded its orders.

“No,” Sava said.

“You haven't read it.”

“I know what it is.”

“I'm not asking to fight.”

“You're asking to go with those who fight and turn what they do into history.”

“Someone has to write the truth.”

“Everyone says that before finding out who pays for their food.”

Nemi turned her head face-on.

“You worked for the Chamber of Measure.”

Sava felt the blow and, at the same time, pride that her daughter had found it.

“That's precisely why I know.”

“Uncle Taren approved.”

“Your uncle doesn't decide for you.”

“You do?”

“Until you're an adult.”

“In the lower chambers, people my age already have children.”

“And half of them have ruined bodies before they're twenty.”

“But they existed without asking permission.”

Sava took the application. Beneath Taren's signature was a note: ARCHIVE ONLY, NO FRONT LINE. A precaution that reassured and infuriated her equally.

“A month's trial,” she said. “You stay in Vaor.”

After the trial month was approved, Nemi hugged her so quickly Sava had no time to prepare her posture. Then she left to receive her Memory cloak.

Sava remained alone in the office. From a drawer she took her old Chamber of Measure badge and Oru's green ribbon. One represented the institution that had given her the language of limits; the other, the man who had taught her that living systems did not respect limits merely because they were well calculated.

On the surface, the tower's inauguration gongs began to sound. The floor carried them down to the office. They were neither the three retreat beats nor the Face code. Daran had invented a new rhythm: one long beat, two short, one long. It meant *we stand and are seen*.

Sava put on Oru's cloak and went up.


CHAPTER XIV

THE CHARTER OF CONTINUITY

VESSA AND SAVA · VAOR · THE THIRD YEAR OF THE CONFLICT

The Charter of Continuity was presented as a temporary measure to coordinate the Houses. In practice, it turned crisis occupations into social ranks and momentary advantages into hereditary probabilities.

The tower hall still smelled of heated resin. The wall plates had not fully settled and cracked with every gust. The House representatives pretended not to hear. Admitting that a vertical building unsettled them would mean acknowledging that the old biology survived beneath the new armour.

Before the meeting, Sava visited the communal kitchen. Daran had turned the inauguration into a celebration, and delegates' portions were doubled. In the lower tunnels, the children's quota had been reduced by five per cent after the new recruits arrived. Sava ordered half the official meal sent to the clinics.

The tower administrator looked at her in horror.

“The master approved the menu.”

“The Civil Office approves rations.”

“The guests will notice.”

“Exactly.”

When the meals reached the hall, the representatives found smaller bowls and a note on Vaor's actual consumption. Daran read it, smiled and asked for the note to be preserved in the archive as an example of shared austerity. Sava understood again that he could turn any resistance into part of the spectacle.

In the new tower's hall, Daran had gathered representatives of seven Houses. The tables were arranged in a circle, but his chair stood on a barely perceptibly higher platform. Each delegation had come with its own Face Corps, its own code and its own version of who had first invented the sign.

Taren stood beside the entrance, not beside Daran. Scars whitened his shoulder and thigh. He had worn armour so long that the down beneath the plates no longer grew evenly. Beside him, Vessa held a one-year-old child, Sen. The boy watched the crowd without moving his head in bursts and did not startle when the gong opened the meeting.

Vessa noticed. Sava noticed that she had noticed.

“The markers?” she asked quietly.

“He has five of Taren's seven combinations,” Vessa replied.

“Did you select the embryo?”

“No. I left it.”

“That's still a choice.”

Sen began crying at the second gong. He was not frightened by the sound; he was hungry. Vessa breastfed him while the delegates discussed who deserved reproductive rights. The gesture disturbed them more than the genetic maps. Biology present in the room, with the smell of milk and a child tugging at clothing, made abstractions harder to pronounce.

Daran opened the document.

On the platform, Daran was presenting the Charter of Continuity. The document divided the population into four functions: the Faces, who defended; the Hands, who produced; Memory, who administered and recorded; the Protected, who could not directly sustain the community. Each category had different rations, obligations and reproductive rights.

“You called children, the sick and the elderly ‘the Protected’,” Sava said when her turn came. “It sounds better than dependants.”

“Language's role is to make reality habitable,” Daran replied.

“The Faces receive two birth rights. The Hands one. Memory one every two cycles. The Protected none without an exception.”

“Those who defend continuity must be able to continue it.”

“You select military obedience and call it biological merit.”

A House Tel representative struck the table.

“Our Faces die three times more often than growers. Without additional children, their lines disappear.”

“Perhaps an occupation demanding so many deaths shouldn't be passed on as a privilege,” Sava said.

Daran showed his chisel teeth in a controlled smile.

“Occupations aren't passed on. Useful properties are.”

“Let Vessa speak,” someone said. “She has the data.”

Every head turned towards the doctor.

Vessa put Sen in a carrier and projected genetic maps above the table.

“In twenty-seven months, members of the Face Corps have produced four times more children than the Belt average. Not only because of permits. Partner choice has changed. Families hide births to connect a line to a known fighter. Some Houses offer food for embryos selected by alarm-response markers.”

“Does it work?” the Tel representative asked.

“We don't know what ‘works’ means. A reduced reflex can come with impulsiveness, fragmented sleep, aggression between siblings and lower contextual empathy. You're selecting a package, not a virtue.”

“But it produces fighters.”

“Sometimes. It also produces criminals unafraid of punishment.”

Daran leaned forwards.

“Your recommendation?”

Vessa looked at Taren, then at the child.

“No embryo selection without a public register. No additional rights granted solely by status. Monitoring across three generations.”

“Three generations?” the Orm representative laughed. “Ivarra could attack us in three weeks.”

“Biology won't hurry for your politics.”

“Yes, it will,” Daran said. “Biology has always hurried when death became frequent.”

The discussion did not end with the vote. House Nur's representative, the only one to vote against alongside Sava, asked for an objection to be recorded: categories were to be reviewed annually, and no child could inherit rank before adult assessment. The amendment was formally accepted, then weakened by an annex allowing Houses to offer “early functional education”.

“You've just invented inheritance without naming it,” Sava said.

“We've invented training,” Daran replied.

“A child raised in a tower from the age of three won't have a choice equal to one raised in a workshop.”

“An engineer's child doesn't have the same choice as a musician's either. Families transmit skills.”

“And access.”

“Access is accumulated skill.”

Aven Sor, present as a civil observer, asked who would classify the population.

“Memory,” Daran replied.

Every gaze turned to Sava.

“No,” she said.

“Your office holds the registers.”

“Precisely. An institution distributing rations mustn't also decide the reproductive value of functions.”

“The Chamber of Measure decided both.”

“And the result is why we're here.”

In the end, the Charter created three commissions: one of the Faces, one of the Hands and one of Memory. Each had to approve reclassifications. The Protected had no commission of their own; their interests were presumed to be represented by everyone.

“That means no one,” Vessa said.

No one answered her.

• • •

The first consequences appeared before the electronic ink had settled. A woman named Rema came to the Civil Office with her nine-year-old son. The child had a deformity of the hind limbs and worked excellently with fine instruments. The Charter had classified him as Protected because he did not meet the production standard and could not enter the Corps.

“He was getting an apprenticeship permit in optical repairs,” Rema said. “Now the workshop says the places are for Hands.”

“He can be reclassified according to aptitude,” Sava replied.

“The Face commission requires a community-service test. He can't pass the running test.”

Sava called an official. He confirmed it: the form had been copied from military selection and applied to every category because it was the only standardised test available.

“He repairs lenses,” Sava said. “Why does he have to run?”

“For evacuation,” the official replied.

“During the attack in the square, those who ran killed thirty-six people.”

The official folded his sails, confused. The regulations contained no field for irony.

Sava approved the exception and, the following week, discovered eight hundred more. The Charter had been written to make the world legible in a crisis. Applied to life, every category produced edges people fell into.

Vessa brought her a separate list. Seventeen women had been pressured into bonds with Face officers in exchange for their families' reclassification.

“Pressured by whom?”

“Parents, administrators, sometimes their own partners. There is no written order.”

“Then how do I punish it?”

“You can't. You can remove the reward.”

“The Council won't revoke the additional rights.”

“Then the reward will find bodies.”

Sava looked at Sen, asleep on Vessa's arm.

“You let him carry Taren's markers.”

“Yes.”

“Aren't you afraid they'll make him exactly what the Charter wants?”

Vessa drew her child closer.

“I'm afraid that if I try to make him his father's opposite, I'll still be letting his father decide the shape of his life.”

Within three months, the Charter descended from the council halls into everyday gestures. At first, the categories existed only in registers. Then depots demanded visible signs to distribute rations quickly. The Faces received a frontal clasp of matt metal; the Hands, a green knot at the wrist; Memory, a black slate; the Protected, nothing. Absence of a sign was supposed to mean protection. In practice, it meant every official asked why the person in front of them belonged to no function.

Sava first noticed the change in the water queue. A young fighter arrived after an elderly woman and was called ahead because his patrol left in twenty minutes. The woman accepted without protest. When a second fighter did the same, she lowered her sails. At the third, she left without water.

“Why didn't you wait?” Sava asked her in the gallery.

“Because by the time my turn comes, the next patrol will already be urgent.”

“You have a right to a portion.”

“The right is the place at the back of the queue that no one sees.”

Sava ordered a separate emergency tank. The depot filled it from the general quota. The civilian queue became shorter and the portions smaller. Every correction moved privilege instead of eliminating it.

Schools adopted the Charter faster than the administration. Children were grouped for “functional orientation”: coordination, memory, endurance, manual precision. Teachers said it was not selection but discovery of aptitudes. In the playgrounds, games divided according to signs. Faces' children imitated patrols. Hands' children built the obstacles. Memory's children kept score. Those without a category were rescued, wounded or prisoners.

Rema's son, Arel, returned to the Office with a repaired lens. He had fitted an actuator so small Sava could barely see the joint.

“The workshop took me,” he said.

“Then why do you look upset?”

“The master says I can work, but the apprentices won't let me touch their tables. They say my exception doesn't change my category.”

Sava visited the workshop. The master showed her the admission document and insisted he had respected the decision.

“He gets the same lessons,” he said.

Yet Arel worked in a corner, at a lower table, with old tools. When he needed a part, he had to ask a Hands child to fetch it from the store. No one hit him. No one forbade him to learn. Yet the whole room explained to him, through distances and objects, that his presence was tolerated, not normal.

Sava ordered shared tables and equal access. The following week, six apprentices' parents withdrew their children, saying the workshop had lowered its standards. Daran published a message praising Arel's courage and the Hands' generosity. The child's image appeared on banners. His classmates began calling him *the Exception*.

Rema came to the Office again.

“Take him out of your story,” she said.

“I asked for his name not to be used.”

“They don't need a name. Everyone sees his legs.”

Sava sent an order withdrawing the images. Half had already been copied. Arel stayed at the workshop but stopped speaking during lessons.

Vessa opened a clinic without classification in an old transport gallery. On the door she wrote only: *the body before the function*. On the first day came seven Protected, three Hands hiding wounds to avoid losing shifts, and a Face officer who refused to enter until Vessa turned off the sign outside.

“I don't want to appear in the register with them,” he said.

Vessa examined the infection in his shoulder.

“The infection hasn't read the Charter.”

“My body has to stay fit.”

“Then tell it to stop producing pus for political reasons.”

The officer returned for four more days, each time without his frontal clasp. On the fifth, he brought the clinic medicines confiscated from smugglers. Vessa accepted them, then registered the donation anonymously. The new system made people generous and ashamed at the same time.

The reclassification commissions had stalled. Every case required approval from three functions working at different rhythms. The Faces responded quickly to military requests, the Hands to measurable skills, Memory demanded documents. After two months, there were four thousand appeals.

Sava turned a ceremonial hall into an appeals office. People slept in the queue. A grower asked to become a Hand after twenty years of work, but had no certification because his farm had been unregistered. A Memory woman wanted to relinquish her rank and enter the clinic; the commission feared her departure would set a precedent. Two brothers raised in the same alcove had received different categories, and their parents asked for one to be raised, not the other lowered.

“Everyone says they don't want privileges,” Aven told Sava one night. “They just want their disadvantage corrected. The sum of the corrections is privilege.”

“Then we change the structure, not the cases.”

“With what majority?”

The Faces would not surrender priority while the war continued. The Hands would not surrender access won after generations in which their work had been invisible. Memory considered itself the only barrier against abuse. The Protected still had no representation. Every group could describe its own advantage as historical redress.

In the bonding markets, change was faster and harder to measure. Families displayed their children's function beside age, health and lineage. A Face clasp attracted more invitations than a prosperous farm. Mothers said they sought safety, not rank. Fathers said they wanted partners capable of protecting their grandchildren, not fighter grandchildren. In less than a year, preference became tradition.

One of the women on Vessa's list, Ina, came to the clinic after her family arranged a bond with an Orm commander. She had not been threatened. The commander was polite, healthy and could obtain her brother's reclassification.

“I don't hate him,” Ina said. “That's the part that makes me unsure what's happening to me.”

“Do you want him?”

“Sometimes. At other times, I want what comes around him. At least the Chamber told me I wasn't allowed. Now everyone tells me I'm choosing.”

“You can refuse.”

Ina looked towards the next room, where her brother waited for a prosthetics assessment.

“Can I refuse for him?”

Vessa had no treatment for a political question. She offered her time, contraception and a place to sleep. Two weeks later, Ina accepted the bond. Her brother was reclassified. When Sava tried to investigate, both declared the decision had been free.

Daran showed her the statements.

“You can't turn every choice you dislike into coercion.”

“Nor every reward into freedom.”

“Then define the line.”

Sava could not. The Chamber of Measure had had precise thresholds and produced exact injustices. The Charter left choices apparently open and moved pressure into family, attraction, shame and the future. It was harder to condemn precisely because it worked through people's desires.

At the end of the third cycle of implementation, Daran presented the report: production had increased, recruitment had stabilised, theft had fallen and bonds between Houses had grown. In the graphs, the new order seemed to be healing the city.

Sava attached the appeals queue, clinic consultations and Ina's testimony to the report. The Council filed them under “transition costs”.

Arel brought her a second repaired lens. This time he had engraved his initial inside, where only the technician taking it apart would see it.

“Why there?” Sava asked.

“So it can exist somewhere without being an example.”

• • •

After the meeting, he found Daran on the tower platform, looking out over the Belt. From there, the cities' hidden entrances looked like small stains in the terrain. The sky pressed in from every side.

“You put my name on a law I didn't write,” Taren said.

“Your name is the reason they accept it.”

“Then ask me.”

“I've asked you many times. You always answer as though power were dirt that had accidentally got on your hands.”

“The Charter will create castes.”

“Castes existed. The Chamber of Measure hid them in access, education and postponements. I give them visible obligations.”

“And hereditary privileges.”

“Only if their descendants remain useful.”

Taren looked at the towers being built around them. Small nivari moved on scaffolds, black silhouettes against the red star.

“We made the First Face to protect food.”

On the platform, the wind forced them to speak louder. Beneath them, Vaor was almost invisible: openings in rock, lines of condensation, tiny lights. The tower dominated a city that still lived in the ground.

“The Charter bears my name,” Taren said. “People will think I approved it.”

“People already think every victory belongs to you,” Daran replied. “It's the price of being simple in a complicated world.”

“I'm not simple.”

“Not to yourself. To those below, you're the one who didn't run. Try to lead without using that, and you'll be led by those who use it better.”

Taren looked at the enormous sign on the tower. It had been drawn after Raal's thieves' mark, adopted by Leth's young companions, legalised by Daran and now placed above a caste. No one had asked him for ownership of the symbol. Everyone demanded responsibility from him.

“What do you actually want?” he asked.

Daran leaned against the railing, a gesture no nivar raised in the capital would have made at ease.

“I want a world where Vaor no longer asks permission to continue. I want Houses strong enough to make Ivarra negotiate. I want children who aren't raised to consider survival an administrative favour.”

“And you want to stand above them.”

“Of course. Acknowledged ambition is safer than power that declares itself sacrifice.”

A courier came up without waiting for permission. He had dried blood on his cloak and carried the seal of the Lur community.

The slate he held out contained two hundred and six identities.


CHAPTER XV

THE LAW OF THE FACE

NEMI AND TAREN · LUR · A FEW WEEKS LATER

At Lur, a Face Corps had used the Code's first two rules to justify a mass execution. The crime did not look like a return to barbarity, but like a new institution learning how easily it could carry its virtue on a weapon.

A courier climbed the stairs and handed Taren a slate. The message came from the community of Lur, on House Tel's border. The local Face Corps had entered to confiscate supplies and recruits. The inhabitants had surrendered. Two hundred and six had been executed anyway, accused of hiding weapons.

On the depot wall, the fighters had written the Code's first two rules: no killing after surrender; no pursuing those who flee.

Beneath them, they had lined up the dead people's heads.

“Tel says it's central propaganda,” Daran said.

“Our Memory verified the images.”

“Then we send a commission.”

“I'm sending the First Face.”

Daran looked at him sideways.

“The Corps don't belong to you.”

“Does the Code belong to them less than to me?”

“If you go against the Council's decision, you'll turn a legal dispute into one between armies.”

Taren lowered his armour's visor.

“That's what happens when you give laws blades.”

By evening, six hundred fighters prepared to go with him to Lur.

Sava reached the eastern gate before the detachment left. Nemi wore Memory's black cloak and tried to look taller. The recording slate hung on her chest; the blade remained in her bag, as they had agreed.

“You said she'd stay in Vaor,” Sava told Taren.

“I said archive only, no front line.”

“Lur is the front line.”

“Lur is afterwards.”

“After two hundred and six deaths.”

Nemi intervened:

“That's precisely why I have to go.”

Sava looked at them both. In Taren she saw the brother she had raised and the commander she could no longer protect. In Nemi she saw the child brought out of smoke and a young woman who had learned that adults used protection to retain control.

“You send me a signal at every cycle change,” she said. “You don't enter any unchecked building. If fighting starts, you leave with Memory, not Taren.”

“Yes.”

“Repeat it.”

Nemi repeated it. Sava tightened her cloak straps, then touched the tip of her injured sail. The scar had grown with her and pulled its edge into a slightly asymmetrical angle.

“Don't let being able to look convince you that you have to see everything,” she whispered.

“You taught me that what isn't seen ends up in the wrong field.”

Taren gave the departure signal before Sava could find an answer.

The road to Lur passed through abandoned underground orchards. Traces of old grafts remained on the walls, and in some chambers the roots had grown wild, forming white knots that pulsed slowly. The detachment advanced in groups of twelve. They did not sing. News of the massacre had done what Taren's orders had not always managed: it kept them disciplined through shame.

Nemi walked with three older archivists. One, Par, showed her how to record residual smells and projectile directions. Another told her not to film dead children's faces without their families' consent.

“Why?” she asked. “They're evidence.”

“They were people before they became evidence.”

It was the first Memory rule she liked without challenging it.

• • •

At Lur, the dead had been laid out carefully.

They did not lie where they had fallen. The bodies had been washed, arranged by family and covered in working cloaks. Someone had turned their heads towards the village entrance so that every lateral eye looked at the road along which the judges would come.

Nemi began recording before she was told. She counted two hundred and six bodies, thirty-one of them children. She filmed the empty depot, projectile marks on the walls and the two rules written above the heads. Then she stopped the slate and vomited into the irrigation ditch.

Taren found her there.

“You're going back to Vaor,” he said.

“No.”

“This isn't an archive for a child.”

“That's precisely why it has to be made by someone who still sees what it is.”

Taren looked towards the rows of dead. First Face members stood silently at the settlement's edge. House Tel's Corps had withdrawn to an agricultural fortification three kilometres south and sent word that it had executed saboteurs under wartime conditions.

“Don't go near the fighting,” he said.

“Mother told me the same about the truth.”

“Your mother is right more often than anyone likes.”

Nemi cleaned her mouth and raised the slate.

Nemi returned to the bodies once her stomach settled. She counted again, this time by family. A surviving woman watched her. She had a burn wound on her arm and wore three children's bracelets round her neck.

“Have you come to make us important?” she asked.

“I've come to record.”

“It's the same thing once we're dead.”

“What's your name?”

“Iru. Don't put my name beside the dead. I'm still here.”

Nemi closed the automatic field that linked relatives. Iru showed her the depot. The inhabitants had indeed hidden food, not weapons. When the Tel Corps demanded supplies and twelve recruits, the village refused. A child had found an old projectile and kept it as a toy. It had not been fired. The commander had used it as evidence.

“Why did you write the rules on the wall?” Nemi asked.

“They wrote them,” Iru replied. “Before they began. They said whoever handed over their weapons would live. We handed over the only projectile.”

“And then?”

Iru touched her bracelets.

“Then they decided our smell was a weapon.”

Nemi recorded the sentence. She did not yet know whether it would end up in a report or a song. She understood only that the form in which she preserved it would change who could claim not to have known.

The Tel Corps commander was named Orven Tal, though his House and family used different spellings to claim greater antiquity. He was twenty-one, had three children and a deliberately made scar along his jaw. He had trained his fighters to look face-on even when unnecessary, so half of them seemed to suffer from neck pain.

He received Taren before the fortification with two hundred Faces in line.

“You have no jurisdiction in Tel,” Orven said.

“The Code does.”

“The Code is guidance, not law.”

“You wrote it on the dead people's wall.”

“To show they'd been judged before execution.”

“The children?”

Orven moved one sail.

“The families were hiding projectiles. We couldn't separate those responsible without risk.”

Taren felt the line behind him change. There was no sound, but a thousand limbs redistributed their weight to leap.

“Hand over those who gave the orders,” he said.

“I gave the orders.”

“Then come with us.”

Orven smiled.

“If you take me, House Tel attacks Vaor. If you kill me, every Corps will understand that the First Face is their master, not their model.”

“If I leave, everyone will understand the rules hold only while they cost nothing.”

“Now you sound like Daran.”

Taren did not answer. He raised his hand. The First Face drew its blades.

Orven raised his hand too. The Tel Corps did the same.

The root field between the lines was young. White tips emerged from the soil every half pace. Nemi thought of Oru. He would have seen at once how many cycles of food would be lost if fighting began. Taren and Orven each watched the other's body of fighters. The survivors watched the crop.

Nemi checked the recording she had found again. The sound was faint, obscured by the alarm, but Orven's voice remained clear. Par had told her not to leave the line. Sava had told her to leave if fighting began. Taren had told her not to approach.

Every instruction assumed the adults would recognise the right moment.

She stepped out.

Nemi stepped out from among Memory's members.

“I have the recording of the order,” she said.

Orven turned towards her.

“What order?”

Nemi raised the slate. In the village depot, she had found a burnt communications node. Its local memory preserved a few seconds of sound: Orven's voice demanding “the cleansing of all bearers of Lur scent”, followed by a subordinate's question about children.

The answer was clear.

Leave no roots.

The Tel fighters listened twice. At the third playback, some lowered their weapons.

Orven did not deny it.

“The order was transmitted after a child triggered a projectile,” he said.

“No explosion can be heard,” Nemi replied.

“The recording is incomplete.”

“The dead are complete.”

Orven rushed at her.

Taren intercepted his leap. They collided in the air and fell between the lines. Orven drew a short blade from the sheath on his arm and cut towards Taren's abdomen. Taren twisted; the point opened his armour. He caught Orven's wrist and struck his knee with a hind limb. The bone gave way.

The Tel fighters did not attack. The recording kept repeating from the fallen slate: leave no roots.

Orven lay on the ground, breathing rapidly.

“I surrender,” he said.

Taren raised his blade.

The Code's first rule came to mind in his own voice: no killing after surrender.

Around them, two hundred and six bodies waited for a difference between surrender and escape.

“You'll be tried,” Taren said.

“By whom?”

After Orven surrendered, the Tel fighters were disarmed one by one. Taren allowed neither public humiliation nor marking them. Raal demanded the officers be bound facing forwards, backs to the walls, so they could not use their lateral field. Taren refused.

“Judgement isn't an exercise in fear.”

“For them it was,” Iru said.

Taren looked at her. The woman had the right to demand more than he could allow without turning the trial into revenge. That was the first difficulty of a real law: it had to limit precisely those whose suffering had made it necessary.

The tribunal assembled in the irrigation chamber, the only space large enough. The survivors sat on one side, the accused on the other, and water flowed through channels between them. There was no platform for the judge. Taren stood in the middle.

Aven Sor had come as a procedural witness. He demanded that the facts be separated: who issued the order, who repeated it, who fired, who tried to stop it. Lur wanted a single guilt. Aven divided it into hundreds of decisions.

A seventeen-year-old Tel fighter confessed to shooting two children. He was crying so hard he could barely speak.

“Orven said they carried Lur scent and that any root left behind would produce saboteurs.”

“Did you believe him?” Taren asked.

“I don't know. Everyone was firing.”

“Did you see weapons?”

“I saw someone running.”

Whole bodies tensed in the hall. For their ancestors, flight had been proof of danger. For the Tel Corps, it had become proof of guilt.

Iru demanded all their deaths. Par recorded the request without comment. Nemi watched Taren's hands. They trembled slightly. Not from fear, but from the effort to hold the Code, anger and the political usefulness of punishment in the same place.

The trial lasted until the lights had changed intensity twice.

The trial lasted until the change in orbital light. Lur had not had a tribunal for generations; conflicts had been settled through mediation and quotas. Nemi recorded the testimonies. Taren made every accused person turn their whole body towards those speaking. From this came the Law of the Face's second meaning: not only to meet danger, but not to hide in profile when shown what you had done.

Orven and twelve officers were sentenced to death. Thirty-seven fighters who had carried out the orders received forced labour and the loss of rank rights. The rest of the Tel Corps was disarmed and sent home.

At artificial sunset, Orven was brought into the field.

“I surrendered,” he told Taren.

“Yes.”

“Then you're breaking the Code.”

“The Code stops the fighting. It doesn't cancel judgement.”

“That's a scribe's distinction.”

“All laws are.”

“When you lose, someone will use it against you.”

“Then I hope there's a good recording.”

In Memory's public version, the sentence occupied two paragraphs and a list of names. The hours that had produced it did not fit so easily.

Aven divided the accused into four groups. The first contained the officers who had planned the encirclement, written the rules on the wall and ordered the firing. The second, fighters who had fired after resistance had ended. The third, those who had guarded exits, carried ammunition or repeated orders without seeing the dead directly. In the last group were seventeen people who had refused, hidden children or deliberately left a gallery uncovered.

Lur wanted a single category. To the survivors, all had worn the same clasp and come in the same formation.

Iru struck the list with her palm.

“The one who kept the door closed didn't fire. The children still didn't get out.”

“He'll be judged for what he did,” Aven replied.

“He was one of them.”

“Belonging cannot be a sentence.”

“For us it was.”

Water ran through the channels between the groups. From time to time, someone had to interrupt the testimony to clean the filters of ash brought in from the field. The tribunal had not been designed. It was held inside the infrastructure that had to keep feeding the very community trying to judge.

A Tel fighter named Saro admitted he had guarded the northern gallery. He was eighteen. When the firing began, a woman came out with two children. The order was to send them back. He let them pass, then reported that smoke had prevented them from seeing.

The woman was alive. She stood among the people of Lur and confirmed it. Iru looked at her as though survival were a betrayal of clarity.

“Then this one goes free?” she asked.

Aven replied:

“He took part in the occupation and guarded an exit. But he refused the order when he understood its consequence.”

“After coming with a weapon.”

“Yes.”

“How much good must you do inside a crime to stop being a criminal?”

No one had a number.

Taren sentenced those in the fourth group to repair Lur under civil authority, with the right to leave after a year if the survivors confirmed they had not broken the rules. It was not freedom, and it was not the same punishment as the others'. Iru voted against. The rescued woman voted for.

The cases of the young people who had fired under orders were the hardest. Some cried. Others repeated Orven's language: saboteurs, contamination, continuity of the House. A sixteen-year-old boy said he had seen no children, only running shapes.

Nemi played a recording. In it, his voice could be heard asking whether to fire. An officer answered: “If they're running, they've chosen.” Then the boy fired.

“I didn't decide,” he said after the playback.

“You pressed,” Iru replied.

“I was told if I hesitated, the others would die.”

Taren asked him:

“Did you think the shapes had weapons?”

“I don't know.”

“Did you want to know?”

The boy began crying again. Taren did not stop him or offer words. Compassion could become a way for the judge to ease his own decision.

Aven proposed forced labour, loss of rank and a weapons ban. Iru demanded death. Tel's representatives warned that executing ordinary fighters would make the House a permanent enemy. Raal, standing at the entrance, said any punishment chosen for its effect on future allies was already political.

“All punishments are,” Aven replied.

“Then don't call them justice.”

“We call them law. Justice is what each side believes the law owes it.”

Nemi recorded, but sometimes forgot to watch the battery level. She kept her eyes on Taren. He listened to every testimony face-on, according to the rule he had just created. After hours, his neck muscles trembled. Looking this way was unnatural for a nivar; the lateral field narrowed, and the body could no longer track exits. The Law of the Face required them to sacrifice part of their safety to give attention. Nemi understood why the gesture could become moral. She also understood why it could become spectacle.

Deliberation took place in a separate room. Taren asked Iru, the rescued woman, a Tel representative, Aven and two Memory members to remain. No one represented the dead without an adult connection. Nemi added to the report that thirty-one children had no vote.

For every person to be sentenced, Aven read the act, the evidence and the alternative the person had had. The list of alternatives changed the room. Some officers could have stopped the attack. Some fighters could have fired into the air, left formation or opened an exit. For others, refusal would probably have meant being shot by their own commanders. Taren did not declare them innocent. But he refused to pretend everyone's freedom had been identical.

Orven did not ask for clemency. He asked for all officers to receive the same sentence as him.

“I gave the order,” he said. “If you kill them separately, you turn discipline into guilt only when it loses.”

“Discipline becomes guilt when it carries out a crime,” Taren replied.

“Then your army will stop at every order to debate the world.”

“I hope so.”

Raal laughed from the doorway, without joy.

“Don't hope too hard. Armies that debate lose to those that don't.”

“Then we have to win slowly enough to remain something else.”

At the end, the verdict was read three times: once for the hall, once for the recording and once in the code of beats, for those who could not hear the voices. After every name condemned to death, the floor remained silent. There was no rhythm of approval.

The young man who had shot two children received forced labour. As he was taken out, Iru stood before him. The guards tried to stop her. She asked them to let her.

“Do you know their names?” she asked him.

The boy shook his head.

Iru told him the children's names, their ages, what food they hated and which one lied when afraid. She made him repeat them. At the second name, he vomited. At the third, his voice disappeared.

“You'll work here,” Iru said. “Every day, you'll know them. This isn't forgiveness.”

After the hall, Taren shut himself in a tool room with Aven. Nemi followed them to the door. From inside came only the water in the wall.

“Twelve sentences,” Aven said. “You can appoint an executioner.”

“No.”

“If you carry them out yourself, every death will increase your authority.”

“If I give them to others, I use their anger to keep my own hands clean.”

“There is no clean option.”

“Then I'll choose the one for which I answer.”

When he came out, Taren held the sentencing slate. His fingers trembled enough to make the text move.

Nemi asked him:

“Are you afraid?”

“Yes.”

“Of them?”

Taren looked towards the field where the execution was being prepared.

“Of how quickly the others will call this power.”

Orven's execution took place in the field, but Taren forbade a crowd. Present were those survivors who wished to attend, Tel's representatives, Memory and the doctor. Nemi held the slate. She could have framed only the condemned man and the blade, but widened the shot: the destroyed roots, Iru, the disarmed fighters, Taren.

Orven asked for water. Iru gave it to him. The gesture angered him more than refusal would have.

“Do you pity me?”

“No,” she said. “I don't want your thirst to become the last thing your followers tell stories about.”

After the sentence was carried out, Taren remained beside the body. He did not look at the slate.

“Did you record it?” he asked.

“Everything.”

“Including the surrender?”

“Yes.”

“Including the trial?”

“Yes.”

“Then keep them together.”

“So people can see you were right?”

Taren turned his head towards her.

“So they can say when I wasn't.”

• • •

On the night after the trial, Lur did not celebrate. People buried their dead in the crop trenches, mixing ash with soil. First Face members helped without armour. Some Tel fighters sentenced to labour dug beside them. No one knew how to rebuild a community after the law had avenged it partially and too late.

Nemi entered the depot to copy the weapons inventory. A thermal cell's serial number had been unevenly erased. Three characters remained beneath the burnt layer.

She recognised them from Vaor's archive.

The Veyr armoury.


CHAPTER XVI

DARAN VEYR

TAREN · VAOR · AFTER RETURNING FROM LUR

The evidence found at Lur connected the Night gangs' weapons, the genetic museum key and the crisis's first provocations to House Veyr. The question was no longer whether Daran had manipulated events, but how much of the new order had been deliberately built.

Nemi found the evidence against Daran in the confiscated weapons inventory.

The Tel Corps projectiles had erased serial numbers, but one thermal cell retained its manufacturing signature. It had been made in the Veyr armoury three years before the storm. Two other batches had been transferred to a coded beneficiary. Nemi compared the code with the slate taken from Raal after the depot fight.

It was the same.

Raal studied the data for a long time.

“An intermediary gave us the weapons,” he said. “He asked us to hit depots and leave the sign. He said Daran would be forced to recognise the settlements if we became a big enough problem.”

“Did you know who was paying him?”

“I didn't ask. Hunger is a form of obedience.”

Nemi searched further. House Tel's archive contained an old access key to Ivarra's Genetic Museum, transmitted through the same intermediary. The identity used at its last activation was Reu Tal, the dead curator.

Raal asked for his confession not to be recorded. Nemi refused.

“Then I won't talk,” he said.

“You've already talked about the intermediary.”

“To Taren, not to history.”

They were in a small room in Lur. The slate lay on the table, its recording light visible. Taren stood beside the door.

“Turn it off,” he told Nemi.

“Why?”

“Because we need to know.”

“And the others don't?”

“They'll know after we can verify it.”

Nemi kept her eyes on him. It was the first time Taren had asked her to limit memory for an immediate advantage.

“If Daran dies before verification, who decides what Raal said?”

Raal gave a short laugh.

“Your archive child caught you.”

Taren approached the slate and switched it off himself.

“Speak. Then we'll repeat on the record what you can substantiate.”

Raal described the intermediary: a smell of preservative, a central accent, one sail cut at the tip. He had not seen his face. He had received projectiles, depot codes and the promise that attacks would force recognition of the communities. At first, he ordered thefts without casualties. After a guard refused the code, a gang member killed him. The intermediary paid extra.

“Why did you continue?” Nemi asked.

“Because after the first death, we could no longer go to Daran as victims. We had to become dangerous enough for him to negotiate.”

“And the sign?”

“He gave it to us drawn on masks. He said it meant we no longer turned our profiles towards those who erased us.”

The sign the First Face wore as an oath had been designed as an instrument of provocation. Raal saw the change on Taren's face.

“Symbols don't remain the property of whoever invents them,” he said. “Otherwise Daran would still control everything we've become.”

“Perhaps he does,” Nemi replied. “Only from a distance.”

• • •

In Vaor, Daran had learned before they arrived. He closed the gates and summoned his personal guards, but did not arrest the population or attack the Civil Office. He invited Sava to the tower.

Vessa insisted on coming with her and brought Sen. Daran received them in the council hall, without armour. Copies of the evidence lay on the table.

“Nemi is good,” he said. “Better than many of Memory's adults.”

“Where are my people?” Sava asked.

“The Office is functioning. Aven is there. This isn't a coup against the administration.”

“Only against Taren.”

“Against the void that follows if he comes here convinced truth gives him the right to destroy the only political structure we have.”

Vessa held Sen tightly. The child watched the House sign on the wall.

“You released the saryx,” she said.

“One specimen was supposed to be photographed in the reservoir. No child was supposed to be hurt.”

“You brought predators into a city without a real evacuation plan.”

“The plan existed. It had worked in ceremonies for four centuries.”

Sava looked at him with disgust.

“You knew it didn't work. That was precisely the demonstration.”

Daran did not deny it.

“Ivarra didn't respond to reports. It responded to fear. Vaor needed the centre to feel its own body vulnerable.”

“Thirty-seven dead.”

“How many slow deaths were avoided after the population was recognised? How many births received food? How many pipes are guarded now?”

“You can't subtract one corpse from another.”

“That's exactly what the Chamber did every day. I made the calculation visible.”

Sava approached the table.

“No. At least the Chamber pretended every loss was a problem. You chose actual people you didn't know and turned them into a cost.”

Daran turned his attention to Sen.

“This child exists because I made the cost impossible to ignore.”

Vessa bared her chisel teeth.

“Say that again and you'll learn Taren isn't the only one with a delayed reflex.”

For a moment, Daran seemed amused. Then fatigue surfaced.

“Taren will kill me,” he said.

“Perhaps he'll try you,” Sava replied.

“No. At Lur, he needed a trial to create law. Here, he needs my death to create succession.”

“You consider yourself too important.”

“Everyone who gets high enough becomes important through the consequences of their fall.”

He led them into a side apartment and closed the door. It was not a cell, but the guards stayed outside.

“I won't hurt you,” he said. “When he comes, you'll be proof I could still choose limits.”

Sava thought of all the rulers who chose their own evidence of morality.

The apartment had two beds, a table, hot water and a narrow window onto the surface. Daran had imprisoned them in comfort, allowing himself to consider the gesture moderate. Vessa checked the walls, air vents and food. She found no toxins. Only an external lock.

Sen wanted to touch the window. Vessa let him. The child pressed his palms against the cold material and watched the mineral snow carried by the wind. He did not understand that the tower was surrounded or that his father was climbing towards them. To him, captivity was a new room with light.

Sava sat on the floor. The generators' vibration came from beneath the bed. Their rhythm was slightly uneven: two equal pulses and one delayed. Oru would have identified the pump by sound. The thought came so naturally that for a moment she turned towards him.

“Daran wants Taren to kill him,” Vessa said.

“He wants whatever Taren does to confirm his story.”

“Then what should he do?”

“Arrest him, publish the evidence and leave it to a tribunal outside Vaor.”

“Which tribunal? Ivarra? The Houses Daran made powerful? Lur, which follows Taren?”

Sava did not answer. The law was clear only when an institution existed outside the conflict. Daran had spent years destroying precisely that outside.

Sen turned from the window and struck the floor with his heel. Once, then twice. It was the rhythm he used to call Taren home. Vessa picked him up.

“He can't hear you.”

The child repeated it.

A single answering beat came from the corridor.

Vessa turned towards the door. Sava raised her sails. A few seconds later, the lock opened and Daran entered alone. He wore no visible weapon. He carried an old archive box.

“It isn't Taren,” he told Sen.

The child looked at him, then struck the same rhythm again, as though the adult's identity could be corrected through persistence.

Daran put the box on the table. It contained the Chamber's replies to Vaor's requests: migration refusals, reduced quotas, birth warnings, reports describing famine as statistical deviation. Sava recognised some signatures. Two were hers.

“Did you keep them for the trial?” she asked.

“For the succession.”

“You imagine the files absolve you.”

“No. I imagine they explain how a person can come to consider thirty-seven deaths a lever.”

“Explanation isn't a limit.”

“Neither is condemnation.”

Daran took out an old report. Vaor had requested expanded birth rights after losing two crops. The Chamber had approved the relocation of forty thousand people, although there was no real place for them in the centre. Sava had worked on the model. She remembered the discussion as a capacity problem, not a sentence.

“I didn't call you here to make you guilty,” Daran said. “Individual guilt is systems' favourite instrument. It lets you find a person, punish them and preserve the geometry.”

“And you changed the geometry with corpses.”

“I changed what the population admired.”

Vessa tightened her arms around Sen.

“Say it plainly.”

Daran sat. Without the stiff cloak and the hall, he looked smaller than his public image.

“Nivari survived because they fled together. Then we built a civilisation where anyone who didn't run at the signal was treated. Every community refusing redistribution was corrected. Every local ambition was absorbed into commissions. We called it peace, but it was also selection: for generations, we rewarded submission, caution, authorised fertility and the ability to endure a life without visible risk.”

“And you decided to select the opposite,” Sava said.

“I decided a society that can no longer produce people capable of staying will be administered into extinction by the first system claiming to know where the shelter is.”

“You released predators to create heroes.”

“I created a situation the ceremony pretended to represent. Taren already existed. Raal existed. Leth existed. I made the others see them.”

“And those who died?”

Daran looked towards the window.

“They're the part no argument repairs.”

For the first time, he did not immediately try to turn them into a useful cost. His silence was not sufficient remorse. But it was a crack.

“Then open the door,” Vessa said. “Let us stop Taren before he kills you.”

“If you stop him, the Houses will keep me here until the next crisis. If Ivarra tries me, I'll become Vaor's martyr. If Vaor tries me, every piece of evidence will be a vote on the Houses' future. There is no sentence left that doesn't build something.”

“There is staying alive and answering,” Sava said.

“To you, that's morality. To me, it's only another architecture of consequences.”

He stood and pushed the box towards her.

“Publish everything. Your signatures too. My orders too. Don't let Taren receive a simple story.”

“Why are you giving me the archive?”

“Because he'll win. And you're the only person in the room who loves him enough to make his victory difficult.”

Daran stopped beside the door. Sen held out his hand, attracted by the metal clasp at his collar. Daran gave it to him. The child put it in his mouth.

Vessa immediately took it out.

“It isn't clean.”

“Nothing in the tower is,” Daran said.

After he left, Sava opened the box and began copying. She did not know whether she was carrying out his final strategy or limiting it. Often, resistance to Daran used exactly the tools he had placed within reach.

• • •

They returned to Vaor without flags.

The city gates were closed. On Daran's tower, the frontal sign had been replaced by House Veyr's emblem: three roots around a stone. The entrance guards belonged not to the First Face, but to Daran's personal agents.

“Sava and Vessa?” Taren asked.

“Safe,” the gate captain replied. “The master awaits you alone.”

Raal drew his blade.

Taren stopped him.

“If I haven't come out by the third change, open the gates.”

“How?”

“You've spent half your life entering places that didn't want you. I won't insult you with instructions.”

Daran waited at the tower's top. Beneath the open sky, the wind lifted his cloak. Three objects lay on the table: the genetic museum key, a thermal cell and the carbon rod with which Taren had killed the first saryx. Daran had recovered it from the capital's agents.

“Nemi is efficient,” he said. “Sava should be proud.”

“Where are they?”

“In the apartments. Unharmed. Sen is asleep.”

Taren put the evidence slate on the table.

“You released the creatures.”

“I authorised the transfer. My agent was supposed to leave them in the reservoirs beneath the square and send the evidence to the press after the Festival. A seal failed early.”

“Thirty-seven dead.”

“And eighty thousand lives recognised in Vaor. A demographic reform Ivarra had postponed for two generations. Sometimes a door opens only if something tries to eat it.”

Climbing the tower forced Taren to slow down. The old wound in his thigh hurt at every step, and wind came through openings not yet closed. On the fifth level, he found two Veyr guards. They laid down their weapons without a fight.

“Why aren't you stopping us?” he asked.

One avoided his frontal gaze.

“The master said the choice must seem yours.”

Daran had prepared even resistance through its absence.

On the platform, the carbon rod lay beside the key and the cell. Taren recognised it by the crack made by the saryx's jaw. The object had been cleaned, polished and mounted on a ceremonial stand. His first improvisation had become a relic before he turned twenty-one.

“I created the problem the Chamber refused to see.”

“You created Leth.”

“Leth created himself when he ran in a straight line.”

Taren struck him.

Daran fell across the table and laughed with blood on his chisel teeth.

“At last,” he said. “Without a speech.”

He rose and took a thermal needle from his sleeve. Taren dodged; the needle burned the edge of one sail. Daran fired again. He was no fighter, but had had years to study how Taren fought. He drove him towards the tower's edge, where the reflex to retreat from the void should have paralysed him.

Taren did not retreat.

He moved within the weapon's range. The needle pierced his shoulder. He caught Daran's hand and pulled him close.

“You were right about one thing,” he said. “The dead don't keep their reasons.”

Daran smiled.

“Then use them well.”

Taren threw him to the ground. He could have disarmed him. He could have tried him. He could have offered him the Law of the Face he had applied at Lur.

Daran reached for the second needle hidden at his ankle.

The fight was brief, yet Taren lived it in details. The needle's metallic smell. The tower's edge in his lateral field. Daran's foot slipping in blood. The smile after the first blow. Daran was not merely trying to win; he was trying to push him towards the exact gesture that made the new order inevitable.

When his hand reached the second needle, Taren could still have broken his wrist. He saw the option. He understood it. Then he broke his neck.

The sound was small. Daran's body relaxed at once, like any dead animal's. There was no justification in it, no ambition, no theory about lives saved. Taren remained with his hands on him and waited for a feeling of truth like the one in the square.

It did not come.

In its place came the silence of a mechanism that had lost its central part and did not yet know in which direction it would fall.

The wind moved the edge of the dead man's cloak. Taren detached the needle from his ankle and put it beside the first. Two small weapons, hidden in a body that had spoken all its life about structures. If he showed them to the crowd, the death would seem defensive. If he did not, opponents would call it an execution. Both were true, but one was more useful.

Nemi reached the platform first. Her slate was already recording. She looked at the body, the blood and Taren's hands.

“Don't stop,” he said.

She had not stopped.

The Veyr guards came up in small groups. Some laid down their weapons. Others knelt beside Daran. No one tried to attack Taren. The absence of resistance frightened him more than the fight. Daran had told them what to do after his death, or had built them a world where the gesture came by itself.

The guard captain asked:

“Who gives orders now?”

Taren looked at Nemi. The question was already recorded.

“No one until the gates are open and the Council assembles.”

“Without an order, people will enter the tower.”

“Then block the stairs, not the city.”

“Under what authority?”

Taren almost said *mine*. The word formed before he could stop it. He replaced it.

“Under the construction safety rule.”

The captain accepted. The new age's first command hid inside a technical regulation.

In the council chamber, they found open registers and messages prepared for every outcome. If Daran killed Taren, the communiqué spoke of defending continuity. If Taren arrested him, House Veyr demanded a public trial. If he killed him, the message invited the Houses to recognise the First Face as military successor. Every version had seals, recipient lists and ceremonial texts.

“He wrote your speech too,” Nemi said.

A slate bore the sentence: *I did not seek power, but I will not flee its burden.*

Taren read it twice. It was exactly what he would have said.

“Delete it.”

“From the system or the archive?”

“From the system. Keep it in the archive.”

In the bottom drawer, they found unsent letters to Sava, reports on Sen, commanders' assessments and a map of the Houses marked by how quickly they would submit to a symbol. Daran had left no single plan. He had left the possibility that whoever won would continue thinking in his categories.

Taren closed the drawer.

“Open the gates,” he said.

“I can publish all this now,” Nemi replied.

Below, the crowd was already beating his name. The sound rose through the tower's skeleton and reached their soles.

“If you publish now, they'll think I'm justifying his death.”

“If I wait, they'll think you chose what to show them.”

“Then write down the time. Write that I asked you to wait. Publish after the Council, whatever I say.”

Nemi entered the order. It was the first time her uncle had given Memory an instruction that could hurt him and insisted it remain visible.

Taren descended the stairs with Daran's blood on his hands. At every level, guards and Face members stepped aside. Some looked at him with gratitude. Others with fear. For all of them, the centre of power had already moved into his body before any law recognised it.

• • •

When the gates opened, no one knew whether Daran's agents had abandoned them or Raal had found the mechanism. The First Face entered without a fight. Sava came to the tower courtyard with Sen in her arms and Vessa beside her.

“Did you kill him?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Had he surrendered?”

Taren looked at the body above.

“No.”

Nemi kept the slate running. She did not lower it.

Vaor's Council assembled before mid-cycle. The representatives of the Hands, Memory and the Corps asked Taren to take over the administration until a Veyr successor was chosen.

“I don't belong to the House,” he said.

“That's why the other Houses want you,” Raal replied.

“I don't want the tower.”

Sava kept her eyes on him.

“Power isn't an object you can refuse after killing the person who held it.”

Taren sat on Daran's platform.

Not because he wanted it. Because everyone else remained standing and waiting.

Raal struck the floor once.

“The First Face,” he said.

The hall repeated the title. It no longer designated the unit. It designated the person.

Before the Council, Sava found him alone in a service room. Vessa was treating his shoulder wound, and Nemi stood in the doorway with the slate running.

“Did you have a choice?” Sava asked.

“Yes.”

The answer stopped her. She had expected an explanation, the hidden needle, the threat to them.

“And you chose to kill him.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Taren looked towards Nemi. The slate's light was recording him.

“Because he was going to fire again. Because I hated him. Because a trial would have divided Vaor. Because part of me knew the hall below would follow me afterwards.”

Nemi did not lower the device. Vessa tightened the bandage.

“Is this the statement?” Sava asked.

“It's what I can say without turning one cause into the convenient story.”

“Daran would have made you leader even if you arrested him.”

“I know.”

“Then his death doesn't free you from what he built.”

“I know.”

Sava sat. For a moment, they were again the two children after the tunnel collapse: she trying to decide what came next, he watching the fire too long.

“When you enter that hall,” she said, “don't say you don't want power. It's exactly the sentence Daran prepared for you.”

“What should I say?”

“That you took it. That it can be taken from you. And build something that makes that possible before you begin believing only you can hold the whole world together.”

Taren looked at the bandage, then at the Face sign on his cloak.

“Will you help me?”

“I'll obstruct you.”

“That's not the same thing.”

“In healthy government, it should come close.”

In the Council hall, when the title First Face was repeated, Taren sat on the platform.

This time he said:

“I accept leadership of Vaor until we establish under what law you can remove me.”

The crowd struck the floor. Few heard the second half.


PART IV

THE SKY WITHOUT A CEILING

• • •


CHAPTER XVII

THE WHITE WINTER

SAVA AND SEN · VAOR · SIX YEARS AFTER DARAN'S DEATH

The White Winter came not from climate, but from administration. The orbital mirrors were moved so little that every order seemed technical; after a few months, thousands of families walked east with the ice behind them.

Six years after Daran's death, the light moved.

It did not go out abruptly. The orbital mirrors changed angle by fractions so small that only instruments noticed at first. The Belt's fertile band slid east. Ivarra and the loyal domains received nine more minutes of light each cycle. Vaor, Lur, Orm and the settlements without a House each lost two.

After a month, the soil cooled.

After two, the agricultural bacteria slowed.

After three, the Night's ice entered the first pipes.

Seln Aru called the phenomenon an orbital safety correction. The Great Hearing published models showing instability in the mirror swarm and advised western populations to redistribute temporarily towards the centre. No one uttered the word punishment.

In Vaor, snow covered the bases of the towers.

In Taren's first year of leadership, the position of First Face had been provisional. In the second, the Council renewed it because of local wars. In the third, the Houses requested a five-year mandate to provide “strategic continuity”. Taren accepted after Sava imposed a formal right of removal by three-fifths of the functions. No removal vote had been initiated. The Faces considered him indispensable, the Hands feared the chaos of succession, Memory needed access to military archives, and the Protected had only just formed their representation.

Power had not become hereditary. Time, institutions and fear had simply arranged themselves so the alternative seemed ever more expensive.

Taren no longer lived in Daran's tower. He had turned the upper platform into a coordination hall and slept in a low room beneath the barracks, without windows. Vessa said it was his way of rejecting the symbol while retaining all its commands.

“If you want to prove you aren't the tower's master, move the Council into the workshop,” she had told him.

“The map doesn't fit there.”

“The map fits anywhere. The commanders don't want to sit on the floor beside workers.”

Taren moved two meetings. At the third, the Houses came with their own chairs and guards. A month later, the Council returned to the tower.

The six years had not been quiet. They had merely become organised enough for violence to receive meetings, budgets and deadlines.

In the first year, Sava tried to use the right of removal. Taren had confiscated two House Tel shipments after it refused to send food to a community without a House. The confiscation saved the community's crop and violated the Charter's procedure. Sava requested a vote not because she wanted to remove him, but because an unused rule was not a limit.

The Council met in the workshop at Taren's insistence. The Faces came in armour. The Hands came straight from their shift and smelled of nutrient. Memory projected the complete order. For the first time, the Protected had sent three representatives, one of them Rema, Arel's mother.

“If we remove him, who leads the patrols?” House Orm asked.

“Someone else,” Sava replied.

“Name them.”

There was no name the Houses could agree on. The vote obtained fifty-nine per cent, one point below the threshold. Taren remained.

That evening, the channels announced that the First Face had won a confidence vote. Sava demanded a correction: it had been a failed removal attempt. The correction appeared under the headline “Leadership accepts democratic oversight”. The mechanism meant to show Taren could be removed had demonstrated to the public exactly how hard it was.

“Did you vote against me?” he asked her.

“Yes.”

“Did you think I should go?”

“I thought the possibility had to be real.”

“And if it had succeeded?”

“Then we'd have learned whether we'd built institutions or only dependence.”

Taren did not get angry. That worried her more. He had begun to receive Sava's opposition as proof of his own tolerance.

In the second year, the community of Hes asked to become a House. The refugees no longer wanted to depend on the old families' votes and believed a name would give them land, a Council seat and the right to a Corps. Sava opposed it. Daran was dead, but his model reproduced without him.

“We don't want old blood,” said the woman who had led the agricultural restoration. “We'll elect leadership for five years.”

“And after five?”

“We elect again.”

She was the first leader. The second election never took place: as the term approached, Hes's border was attacked, and the Council extended the mandate for continuity. Her daughter led the defence. The families preferred her as successor because she knew her mother's codes and had been present at negotiations. No one declared the position hereditary. Every practical reason simply settled in the same family.

In the third year, Sen turned four and received his first public assessment. Vessa had refused the neural testing required of Faces' children. Daran no longer existed, but military clinics had kept the protocol. Parents wanted it because good results opened command schools.

“Don't I want to know whether he has Taren's reflex?” Sava asked her.

“I want to know when he's ill,” Vessa replied. “I don't want every peculiarity to become an invitation to a function.”

“If he has it, it'll be noticed anyway.”

“Then at least let him be noticed as a person before being measured as an inheritance.”

At the children's ceremony, Sen did not startle when a gong accidentally fell. The crowd fell silent, then began striking the floor. The child was frightened by their reaction and began to cry. In the images distributed later, the crying had been cut. Only the boy standing still before the sound remained.

Other parents began requesting partners from families with “frontal stability”. The term was not medical. It included lack of flight, robust jaws, rapid healing, fertility and the presence of a relative in the Corps. Vessa published a report showing the markers were weak, contextual and mixed with nutrition. Bonding messages cited the report as proof that stability was “multifactorial” and should therefore be pursued through family rather than a single test.

In the fourth year, the Festival of Retreat was held in Vaor too. Daran had informally banned it, calling it a ritual of central submission. Taren brought it back to show the old peace need not be erased. Children were to recognise the shadow, retreat in order and return after the signal.

At the first rehearsal, half stayed in the circle. Not because they felt no alarm, but because leaving in front of their classmates had become shameful. The instructors praised those who had obeyed the rule. The spectators photographed those who stayed.
