After Raal's death, Sen made no sound. He held his weapon in both hands, and the creature's blood ran down his forearms. The children Raal had pushed towards the exit cried and crowded around the tables. Some looked at Sen as their saviour. None had seen who threw himself in first.

The doctor approached Raal's body. His armour was open from shoulder to abdomen. Raal still breathed, infrequently, through a punctured lung.

“Get them out,” he told Sen.

The boy knelt.

“I'll take you to the doctors.”

“These are doctors.”

“I'll take you to Vessa.”

Raal tried to laugh and coughed blood.

“Don't turn every dead person into an impossible task. Get the children out.”

“Why did you do that?”

“Because you were in the way.”

Sen shook his head angrily.

“Tell me the truth.”

“That is it. I pushed you. Now push them.”

Raal moved his remaining intact sail towards the children once. Then his body relaxed. The doctor checked his pupils and closed his mouth.

Sen stayed beside him until one of the children tugged at his cloak.

“Is another one coming?”

There was no other saryx. But shots came from the corridors, and smoke descended through the ceiling. Sen stood. He paired the children, counted them twice and led them towards evacuation. He spoke in Taren's voice, briefly and without promises. He did exactly what Raal had asked. Later, he would remember only that he had killed the creature.

In the Low Square, the gates opened under the explosion's pressure. Air from the outer tunnels entered like a dirty wind, carrying the camp's smell and the Houses' shouts. The first units crossed without waiting for an order. Taren tried to form civilian corridors, but the square filled with signs, weapons and people searching for relatives.

A woman from the Gardens recognised her brother in the Tel Corps. She ran towards him, and a central agent mistook her for an attacker. The projectile broke her knee. Her brother killed the agent before he could explain. In another corner, two Orm families refused to leave with the unit that had come to free them. The commander ordered them taken “for safety”.

Taren understood that opening was not an event. It was the beginning of a struggle over every freed person.

“Vaor units, protect exits and hospitals!” he beat through the ground. “No requisitions! No families moved by force!”

The signal repeated, but the other Corps transmitted their own orders. The square had become a language with too many speakers.

Near the old Retreat monument, they found Raal's body being carried on a stretcher and Sen walking beside it. Taren saw the blood, the weapon and the burn on the boy's arm. For a moment, he did not understand which body was his son's. Then Sen looked up.

“I got the children out,” he said.

Taren touched his head, a gesture too small for what he felt and too public for what the boy needed.

“Raal?”

Sen looked at the stretcher.

“The saryx was Seln's.”

Taren recognised the beginning of a story that could justify anything that followed. He caught him by the shoulders.

“Raal chose to push you away and asked you to get the children out. We'll learn the rest.”

“I killed him.”

“You killed the animal.”

“It's the same thing.”

“No.”

Sen pulled his shoulders free.

“For you, everything becomes different when you have to choose who bears the blame.”

He went towards the doctors. Taren stayed beside Raal while the army passed through the gates and civilians ran in both directions.

He had no time to mourn him. The first looting report came from the south. House Tel had occupied a food depot and closed the doors with people inside. Orm claimed the transport station. A Corps without a House had raised its own flag over a school and declared the neighbourhood free.

Taren opened the general channel.

“The objective is the Chamber of Measure. All units remain in their assigned sectors.”

The Tel commander answered after a pause.

“Your objective. Ours began after the gates opened.”

In the city centre, Nemi had reached the Chamber entrance. Sava and Seln waited for her. Hale leaned against the wall, almost unconscious. Ventilation fluctuated. On the map, each colour of the alliance spread through Ivarra like a separate infection.

Taren set off towards them with Sen and the rest of the First Face. Behind them, three thousand dead, wounded and missing people were already beginning to receive reasons.


CHAPTER XXI

THE CUSTODIAN

TAREN, SAVA AND NEMI · THE CHAMBER OF MEASURE

The Custodian did not lose power when the army entered the Chamber. He lost it only after forcing the victors to choose which instruments to keep. In that room, the new order was born from an emergency no one had time to define.

Nemi reached the Chamber of Measure and opened the last door with Daran's key.

Inside were Sava and Seln Aru.

The Custodian wore no armour. On the table stood two cups of infusion and the Ilen–Ven family's closed file.

“Taren is near,” Nemi said.

“I know. The Great Hearing counts his steps.”

“Then transfer the keys.”

“When he enters.”

“Why wait?”

Seln looked at the city map, full of red dots.

“Because victory is the last moment a conqueror can still say everything was necessary. Afterwards, he begins to choose.”

The door opened.

Taren entered covered in blood from several sides. Behind him came Sen, a burn across half his face and Raal's broken necklace clenched in his hand.

Seln Aru stood.

“Now,” he said, “we can measure what you've become.”

Taren did not answer. He closed the door behind him, and for a moment the city's noise became a distant vibration. The Chamber of Measure had been designed to soothe. Its curved walls had no edges, light came from below, and the air retained the faint smell of herbs used in birth centres. The blood on his armour looked darker in that light.

Nemi saw him scan the exits, ventilation openings, the place from which Seln could release gas. He did not look at the Custodian as a man. He looked at the Chamber as a creature.

“The systems are in civil mode,” Sava said. “No veyrin in the pipes.”

“How do you know?”

“I checked.”

“And if the check depends on him?”

Seln raised one cup.

“The infusion isn't poisoned either. But I won't insult you by asking you to believe it.”

Sen stepped towards the table. The burn ran from his temple to beneath his jaw; his down had shrivelled into black edges. A doctor had applied a transparent membrane, but the boy had torn it off. In his other hand, he held Raal's necklace so tightly the metal had cut his palm.

Sava approached.

“Let me see the wound.”

“Not now.”

“Now, precisely.”

Sen moved back. Taren gestured for him to let her. The boy agreed only because the gesture could be interpreted as an order. Sava cleaned the burn with sterile water. He did not flinch. Not because it did not hurt; his jaw was clenched, and his fingers around the necklace had turned white. But he had practised stillness before pain for years.

“Raal would have made noise,” Sava said.

Sen looked at her.

“What?”

“He swore when we treated him. Said ignored pain becomes a commander.”

“He didn't say that.”

“He said it to me, at the Lake.”

The boy loosened his grip a little. Sava fixed the membrane back in place. Around them, the adults waited for him to become a symbol or a child again. For a few moments, he remained only wounded.

Hale had collapsed beside the door. Nemi called the doctors and handed them the slate with the map. As they lifted him, he caught her wrist.

“Don't let the gates close again.”

“We won't.”

“That's not the same as keeping them open.”

Then he fainted. Nemi thought that in a few hours they had all learned to speak in warnings.

Seln opened the Ilen–Ven family file. It was not a theatrical gesture. The document integrated into the table, and graphs rose into the air: birth rights, assessments, moves, consumption, treatments. Their life reduced to coloured paths.

Seln Aru did not ask who had won.

He looked at the burn on Sen's face, the blood on Taren's armour and Nemi's running slate. Then he opened the Ilen–Ven family file and projected it above the table.

“Four people,” he said. “One authorised birth. One postponed until it became impossible. One individual from the Adaptive Reserve. One death produced by a non-lethal intervention. If I'd wanted to invent an accusation against my system, I wouldn't have constructed one so elegant.”

Taren drew his blade and placed it on the table.

“Transfer control.”

“Not yet.”

Sen took a step. Taren stopped him with his arm.

“The saryx in the Gardens killed Raal,” the boy said.

Seln turned his body towards him.

“It was released when an explosion disabled the museum's locks. I didn't give the order.”

“You kept it there.”

“Yes.”

“Then it's yours.”

“By that logic, every death caused by the First Face belongs to your father.”

Sen drew air between his chisel teeth. His hand went towards his weapon.

Taren caught it.

“Raal told you to get the children out, not find him a reason,” he said.

Sen lowered his gaze. For the first time since entering, his body turned sideways, the old retreat position.

Seln noticed that too.

“You can still teach him,” he said.

“You don't talk about my son.”

“I'm talking about the successor the world is building around him, as it built one around you.”

Taren gripped the table's edge.

“You've watched me since childhood.”

“The Chamber tracks every rare deviation.”

“You sent me to Vaor.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Seln touched the map. Vaor appeared as a network of red chambers, many hidden at the time of the first inspection.

“The models showed three probable outcomes. In the first, Daran was replaced, the population redistributed and the domain survived with controlled losses. In the second, Daran provoked a revolt, and you were killed or arrested before becoming a symbol. In the third, his unit became useful and could be absorbed into a planetary risk service.”

“You didn't foresee the war?”

“I put it below four per cent.”

Nemi laughed without joy.

“Then the Great Hearing understood nothing.”

“It understood behaviours. It didn't understand stories. Daran took a rare individual and gave him a story in which his rarity explained everyone's suffering. After that, every victory rewrote the probabilities.”

Sava approached the map.

“Would you have stopped everything if you'd known?”

Seln thought before answering.

“I would have tried.”

“How? Another postponement? Sterilising Taren? More mist?”

“Perhaps.”

Taren bared his chisel teeth.

“And you call me a monster.”

“No. Monsters are simple. You're an institution that still thinks it's a person.”

At the word *institution*, Taren took the blade from the table, turned it and replaced it with the edge towards himself. Sava knew the gesture: he was trying to occupy his hands before anger became action.

“You saw three outcomes,” he said. “In all of them, you used us.”

“In all of them, I tried to reduce loss,” Seln replied.

“That isn't the answer.”

“It's the only one I have.”

Nemi scrolled through the file. She found Sava's first postponement, Taren's assessment, Oru's transfer to Vaor and her own birth. Beside every decision appeared a column of justifications and a column of estimated effects. Actual effects had been added later in red. Oru's death occupied one line: *collateral loss associated with incomplete dispersal under veyrin.*

“Read it,” she told Seln.

The Custodian looked at the line.

“You know what it says.”

“Read it aloud.”

Sava wanted to stop her. Not because Seln needed protection, but because Oru's name did not deserve to become evidence in a confrontation where everyone sought control. Nemi looked at her and understood. Then she pushed the file towards the Custodian anyway.

Seln read the exact wording.

Nemi unfolded her slate and projected her father's last recording: the room with foam, the sound of slowed breathing, Oru's hand searching for a wall that was no longer there. She did not let the image reach his death. She stopped it while he was still trying to rise.

“This is the complete effect,” she said.

Seln did not look away.

“Yes.”

“And you'd still use the mist again?”

“In the exact same conditions, with the same alternatives known then, probably.”

Sen made a short sound. Taren put a hand on his shoulder before he moved.

“Why do we let him talk?” the boy asked.

“Because we want something other than an execution,” Sava replied.

“Raal didn't get a tribunal.”

“Neither did the saryx.”

“Don't talk about him as if he were an animal.”

“About whom?”

Sen stopped. In his mind, Raal and the creature, the Custodian and the cage, death and reason had become too tightly bound. Sava saw the moment when the boy could choose between pain and doctrine. No sentence could make the choice for him.

“Raal received a life,” she said. “And in that life, he did many things that don't fit inside how he died. Don't take away the rest to make hatred easier.”

Sen turned his profile. He neither accepted nor attacked. For the moment, it was enough.

A thread of dust fell from the ceiling. The southern explosion reached the Chamber after a long delay. The map filled with warnings.

An explosion came from the city. The map changed colour in South Root. House Tel's Corps had attacked a depot, and central agents had responded. Three air pipes were damaged.

Nemi opened communications.

“The Tel commander demands control of a mirror and requisition rights. She says without them she'll withdraw her Corps and let the neighbourhood burn.”

Taren activated the channel.

“Stop the attack and hand the depot over to the local administration.”

The commander's face appeared. Blood marked her muzzle, and the Face sign was painted in gold.

“We lost two thousand fighters getting into Ivarra. We aren't going back with relatives and promises.”

“You didn't come for spoils.”

“Perhaps you didn't. The Houses voted for a division after victory.”

“I didn't vote.”

“Then you don't lead the alliance.”

The transmission ended.

Seln looked towards Taren.

“This is the choice I wanted to see. You can keep the army if you give it what it demands. You can keep the city if you turn the First Face against those who brought you here. Either option produces a centre.”

“You constructed the situation.”

“Leadership is the art of receiving situations constructed by others and pretending the remaining choice belongs to you.”

Taren turned to Nemi.

“Can you open every channel in the city?”

“With the Custodian's transfer.”

“Seln.”

The Custodian sat. For the first time, he looked old.

“The Great Hearing has no single button,” he said. “Control is a network of trust. I can transfer the keys to you, to Nemi or to a council. But the system will require a continuity structure. Who decides when you two disagree?”

“The council of functions,” Sava said. “Faces, Hands, Memory and elected representatives of those now called Protected.”

“Four centres in a crisis,” Seln replied. “You'll be paralysed.”

“One centre brought us here.”

“Two centres brought us here,” Nemi said. “Yours and Taren's. Put the keys at five nodes. No major reorientation without three. Local operations remain automatic and public.”

“The Houses will capture the nodes.”

“Then distribute them by function, not territory.”

Taren looked at her.

“Memory gets one.”

“Not me. The archive, with a limited mandate.”

“Mandates expire only if someone can take them away.”

Sava almost smiled. Her daughter answered Seln in her own language.

“The civil node can't remain undefined,” Sava said over the ventilation alarm. “Otherwise the first four functions will appoint for themselves the population they claim to represent.”

“We don't have time for elections,” Taren replied.

“We have time to invent a Custodianship while the city burns.”

“The Custodianship already exists.”

“Exactly.”

Nemi opened the neighbourhood registers. The city had housing councils, parent groups, care associations and representatives of those without a function. None covered the Belt. Yet all were more real than an empty place on a diagram.

“A provisional node of representatives from the affected neighbourhoods,” she said. “A hundred-day mandate. After that, a general assembly.”

Seln slowly shook his head.

“The central neighbourhoods will have communications infrastructure and capture the selection.”

“Then we allocate half by population, half by lot among recognised communities,” Sava said.

“Hidden communities aren't recognised.”

“We recognise them.”

“In one sentence?”

“That's how they were excluded.”

Taren listened and watched air pressure fall on the map. South Root had twenty-eight thousand inhabitants. House Tel held the depot, central agents controlled the pumping station, and fire approached the food reservoirs. Every second of improvised constitution made him more willing to take the key alone.

Seln noticed.

“That's urgency's advantage,” he said. “Not that it persuades people to abandon rules. It persuades them the rule they want can be written later.”

“Be quiet,” Taren said.

“You haven't taken my office yet.”

“Not for that reason.”

On the medical channel, Vessa requested access to the southern neighbourhood. Agents and Tel both refused the corridor, each suspecting the doctors would carry weapons for the other side. Taren opened a direct channel.

“Vessa, you have twelve minutes before we lose ventilation.”

“Then stop the firing.”

“I'm working on it.”

“No, you're working on who will have the right to stop the firing. Meanwhile I have a hundred and thirty wounded in a station without air.”

Taren closed his eyes. Every argument in the Chamber was true, and all became obscene beside the breathing on the channel.

“Seln, transfer the Custodianship to me for six hours. After six hours, the key automatically enters the five-node structure.”

“The system doesn't have that condition.”

“Write it.”

“Not in six minutes.”

Nemi approached the interface.

“I can set an automatic challenge. At six hours, the other four nodes vote on whether to retain the key. Until then, all orders become public with a delay of no more than a minute.”

“A minute could expose troop positions,” Taren said.

“Then you're beginning to choose what transparency means in war.”

“Not now.”

“Now,” Sava repeated.

Taren looked at the three women, then at the map. At last, he nodded.

Nemi entered the condition. The Great Hearing requested validation from the current Custodian. Seln did not yet place his palm.

“I want you to understand what you're doing,” he said. “You aren't dividing power. You're dividing the right to declare that the others are preventing rescue.”

“And you kept that right alone,” Sava replied.

“Yes. Now you'll learn whether the plural makes it less dangerous or only harder to trace.”

The second explosion shook the Chamber.

A second explosion shook the Chamber. Ventilation fell. On the map, the southern neighbourhood was losing pressure.

“Transfer,” Taren said.

Seln placed his palm on the table. The system read his biological signature. Five lights appeared: operational Custodianship, the Chamber of Measure, the Memory Corps, the Hands Network and a civil node still undefined.

“Custodianship to whom?” the system asked.

Seln looked at Taren.

“If you take it, you'll have the power to stop wars between Houses. You'll also have the power to start them without asking.”

“If I don't take it, Tel tears the city apart now.”

“Exactly.”

Taren placed his palm.

“Transfer to Taren Ilen, First Face of the Confederation.”

The key turned red, then white.

At that instant, the city recognised him. Administrative doors answered him, channels opened, repair drones awaited commands. Power did not feel like a crown. It felt like a thousand small things that had stopped telling him no.

Taren opened every speaker.

“House Corps, listen. The Gardens are open. Families are free to leave or stay. The light in the west has been restored. No House receives spoils, territory or orbital control. All units withdraw to designated areas and surrender heavy weapons until the Council is formed.”

The Tel commander answered publicly.

“And if we refuse?”

Taren looked at the map. He could shut off the air to the depot holding her Corps. He could move lights, block doors, stop food. Seln's instruments offered him victory without a blade.

“Then the First Face will remove you,” he said.

“Our brothers against us?”

“You are no one's brothers while you hold civilians between weapons and food.”

In the square, Vaor's members turned south. They had fought beside Tel at the Lake, in winter and at the gates. Now they went against them.

The fighting lasted two more hours. Taren did not stop the air. He lost three hundred people to avoid using a mechanism he had condemned. House Tel surrendered after its commander was wounded. This time, the Code held.

When the key turned white, Taren felt the city not as territory but as a multitude of requests. A blocked door demanded priority. A reservoir lost pressure. Four hundred drones awaited routes. Central agents requested confirmation of the new hierarchy. The Great Hearing offered options ordered by estimated losses.

The first recommendation was simple: close the air to the Tel depot for seven minutes. Probability of surrender, eighty-six per cent. Estimated civilian losses, between twelve and forty-nine.

Taren rejected it.

The second: immobilising foam in every corridor, including the medical station. Lower estimated losses, but wounded people with respiratory injuries would die.

He rejected it.

The third: direct assault through two service galleries. Estimated own losses, between two hundred and six hundred. Civilians, below twenty if Tel did not use them as shields.

“The system teaches you how quickly spending your own people becomes acceptable,” Seln said.

“You're no longer an adviser.”

“No. That's why I can tell the truth without turning it into a recommendation.”

Taren selected the direct assault. He sent orders to the First Face and simultaneously opened the medical corridor. Vessa answered with a single sentence:

“Bring me alive those you can save without killing the others.”

In South Root, Vaor units entered through smoke-filled galleries. Doors now answered Taren's signature, but Tel had mechanically welded some and placed explosives at others. Digital power stopped before a metal bar. The Hands had to cut, pump, carry.

Sen watched the map from the Chamber. Taren had ordered him to stay with Sava, but the boy approached the interface whenever a unit appeared. He recognised names. Many had trained him.

“Send Varo's team through the west,” he said. “The eastern gallery has a blind step.”

Taren checked. The boy was right; training plans used that area.

“How do you know?”

“I was there at the winter festival.”

Taren changed the route. A moment later, the eastern mine exploded without casualties. Sen saw the adults' faces acknowledge his usefulness. The burn, blood and map made him look older. Taren felt the world raising his son before he had time to take him away.

“Enough,” he said. “Go to Vessa.”

“I can help.”

“You have helped.”

“Then why do I have to leave?”

“Because I don't want you learning that the only way to stay in the room is to be indispensable.”

Sen gripped Raal's necklace.

“You stayed.”

“Yes.”

“Then that isn't the lesson you're teaching.”

Sava took his hand. He tried to pull away, then yielded. Before leaving, he looked at the controls again. Not with wonder, but with the hunger of someone shown a language he already understood.

The fighting in the south lasted longer than the Great Hearing estimated. Tel had moved civilians into inner chambers, officially for protection, practically to prevent foam and projectiles. The First Face advanced room by room. Whenever they encountered a family, they formed a corridor, took it out and resumed the attack. The pace gave defenders time to withdraw and prepare the next position.

A Vaor commander requested permission to stop the light and enter with thermal vision.

“The civilians have no eye protection,” Taren replied.

“We're losing people.”

“I know.”

“They know you refuse every advantage. They use our Code as a wall.”

Taren watched the list of the dead grow.

“Then the wall holds for us too.”

He was not sure the sentence made sense to those in the tunnel. It made sense to him in the Chamber, where every option could be executed without smell or scream. Someone had to pay for refusing Seln's instruments. This time, those who had followed him paid.

When House Tel surrendered, three hundred and twenty-seven First Face members had died or were too wounded to fight. Twelve civilians had been killed, nine by Tel's fire, three by the attackers. The commander was brought to the square on a stretcher. She spat at Taren when he came to see her.

“You gave the city our lives to look cleaner than Seln.”

“No,” he said. “I asked you not to hold food and people at gunpoint.”

“And when you hold the city?”

Taren did not answer. She laughed, then lost consciousness.

• • •

When quiet returned, Seln removed his white cloak and placed it on the table.

“I surrender to the Custodianship,” he said.

Sen abruptly raised his head.

Taren looked at the former ruler.

“You'll be tried for the mist, manipulation of the light and detention of families.”

“By the survivors?”

“By a mixed tribunal.”

“A scribe's distinction.”

Taren recognised Orven Tal's phrase.

“All laws are.”

“And if I'm convicted?”

“I won't intervene.”

“Then you've learned a ruler's most important privilege: naming the separation between his decision and the hand that carries it out.”

Sava approached Seln.

“You could be free if you'd ordered the agents to fight to the end.”

“I could be dead. Don't confuse freedom with the last available gesture.”

The new Custodianship's agents came to take him. Taren asked for him to be taken to the Gardens of Kinship until the trial.

Seln smiled for the first time.

“You'll discover why I called them gardens instead of cells,” he said. “A cage becomes easier to keep when the guard can convince himself the prisoner lives well.”

After he left, the Belt map remained open.

Fifteen local conflicts continued. Six domains refused the new Council. Food quotas were insufficient. Children born under the Face Charter were already old enough for training. No key could change all this without hands, orders and someone to decide what an emergency meant.

Taren sat in the Custodian's place.

Sen stayed beside him.

Sava saw the boy looking at the controls with recognition, not wonder.

“Don't let him think they belong to him,” she told Taren.

“They don't belong to me either.”

“I know. That's precisely why they'll be hard to give back.”

After Seln was taken away, no one immediately occupied his place. Central agents awaited Taren's orders, House Corps awaited the division, and civilians awaited word on whether they could go out. Nemi projected a simple instruction in every neighbourhood: *The gates remain open. No person may be moved without consent. Vital services take priority over House claims.*

Then she added: *Any violation can be recorded with the Memory Corps.*

In the first ten minutes, seventeen thousand recordings arrived. Some showed looting, beatings and arrests. Others were family arguments, neighbours taking extra water, lost children. Memory did not have enough people to verify them. Transparency produced more truth than the institution could carry.

Sava went down to the square. She no longer wore the Chamber's cloak; she had left it on the back of Seln's chair. She helped organise missing-person lists. At a medical post, an Ivarra woman asked her to find a son taken by agents three cycles earlier. Elsewhere, a Tel man sought a sister from the Gardens who refused to leave with him. Everyone came with a story in which opening the gates was supposed to resolve something years had made irreversible.

Beside the Retreat monument, Raal's body awaited official identification. He had no relatives in the registers. The Night outcasts claimed he belonged to their community; Vaor called him a founder of the First Face; Sen refused to let anyone remove his necklace. Vessa sat beside the boy.

“We have to clean his body,” she said.

“Not now.”

“The smell will attract contamination.”

“I don't care.”

“He would have. He spent half the winter arguing with doctors about hygiene.”

Sen looked at her disbelievingly.

“He didn't talk about that.”

“With you, he talked about what he thought you needed. With me, he talked about pus and missing teeth.”

She reached towards the necklace. Sen gripped it.

“This stays with me.”

“All right.”

“For ever.”

“You don't have to decide grief's duration on the first day.”

Sen finally began to cry. Silently at first, then with broken breaths that shook his burn. Vessa held his shoulders. People passed and pretended not to see. For a species that had communicated through body orientation for millennia, discretion meant offering someone your profile.

Taren stayed in the Chamber of Measure until after mid-cycle. His temporary key reached its limit. The other four provisional nodes voted to retain it for ten more days because fighting had not stopped. The civil node still did not exist; in its place voted a hastily assembled collection of seventeen neighbourhood representatives. Thirteen approved, four opposed.

“You promised six hours,” Nemi told him.

“I voted against the extension.”

“And you still stay.”

“The rule says I stay.”

“Seln would be proud.”

Taren removed his armour. Beneath it, his body was covered in small cuts, burns and foam stains. Nemi noticed him trembling. Not from fear, but exhaustion and stimulants.

“Go to bed,” she said.

“There are fifteen conflicts.”

“There'll be fourteen when you wake, or sixteen. No state of the world will give you permission on its own to leave the table.”

“Who tells you to sleep?”

“The archive. After forty hours, my error rate rises and someone else must validate the recordings.”

“The Custodianship has no biological limit in its protocol.”

“Then put one in.”

Taren opened the rules. He wrote that any key holder must be replaced after twenty hours of continuous activity, except by a decision signed by three nodes. The system accepted.

“The new order's first law,” Nemi said. “The ruler must sleep.”

“It won't appear in songs.”

“Then perhaps it works.”

Before leaving, Taren went down to Raal. The body had been cleaned and wrapped in a simple membrane. Sen slept beside him, his head on Vessa's knees. Taren sat on the other side. He made no oath and did not promise the dead man his death would change the world. He placed the broken prosthetic beside his hand and stayed until the city's lights entered the night cycle.

Outside, the House armies still beat their signs through the ground. Inside, drones repaired pipes, doctors counted the wounded, families decided whether to leave, and old agents received uniforms without insignia.

Ivarra had been conquered. No one yet knew by whom.


PART V

THE LAW OF THE LIVING

• • •


CHAPTER XXII

THE TRIAL

SAVA · IVARRA · THE FIRST NINE MONTHS OF THE NEW ORDER

Seln's trial was meant to separate the old order from the new. Instead, it showed how many instruments of centralised peace had become indispensable to the very people who defeated it.

Seln Aru's trial lasted nine months and produced six incompatible definitions of peace.

Central agents maintained that veyrin had prevented a lethal attack. Vaor's survivors showed the bodies of those who had died too calm to run. Mirror engineers demonstrated that moving the light had prevented system overload. White Winter refugees described children buried in frozen soil. The Gardens of Kinship families were divided: some demanded punishment, others testified that they feared returning home more than closed gates.

Seln denied no act.

The tribunal had been built in the old census hall. For four centuries, quotas, moves and birth rights had been approved there. Now the officials' tables formed a semicircle, and the place where families had awaited decisions became witness space.

No side had accepted the panel's initial name. The Houses wanted “the Court of Liberation”. The old agents proposed “the Continuity Commission”. Mist survivors demanded “the Judgement of the Dead”. Sava insisted on a weaker formula: Mixed tribunal for acts of administration and conflict. Everyone hated it, confirming for her that it was sufficiently untriumphant.

The panel had one magistrate from Ivarra, one from Vaor, two elected representatives of affected communities and one Memory member; Sava presided. Taren was not part of it. The Custodianship provided security and could execute the sentence, but his key did not open the evidence archive. Nemi had designed the separation and published the code. In the first weeks, people assumed it was a gesture of trust. Later they understood it had been a gesture of distributed distrust.

Seln came without cloak or restraints. He had lived seven months in the Gardens, in a home identical to those occupied by House families. He had access to books, an inner courtyard and a wall that turned opaque when he approached the exit. He had grown thin. Every morning, he tended his own nutrient plants and sent the tribunal notes on procedural errors in his trial.

“Are you preparing your appeal?” Nemi had asked at one hearing.

“I'm preparing the institution that will need to judge someone else after me.”

“You assume you'll remain useful.”

“No. I assume you'll remain capable of mistakes.”

The first month was devoted to the mist in Vaor. Engineers explained that veyrin had been developed for evacuations and riots in enclosed spaces. It reduced aggression, slowed breathing and made groups easy to direct. At official doses, mortality was below one in a thousand. In Vaor, damaged filters, panic, fires and overcrowding had turned the percentage into seventeen hundred bodies.

A defence lawyer presented the alternative model: if agents had entered with lethal weapons, probable deaths were three times higher. The hall murmured. A woman whose partner had died in the mist stood and threw a piece of breathing membrane towards the table.

“He wasn't a probability,” she said.

The Ivarra magistrate demanded her removal. Sava refused.

“If the tribunal can't bear the sentence, it can't evaluate the model,” she said.

The woman remained. At the end, she testified that she too had advised neighbours to enter the chamber where they died, because central instructions said it was safe. When the lawyer asked whether she considered herself responsible, she began crying.

“That's the wrong question,” Sava intervened. “The witness had no access to data withheld by command.”

“Yet she carried out an instruction,” the lawyer said.

Sava looked at Seln. He did not react. The trial was already becoming a struggle over the line between decision and obedience.

In the third month, Mera Sol testified. She was very old; a frame supported her sails, and her voice came through an amplifier. She had been Taren's therapist and signed the Adaptive Reserve recommendation.

“Did you know he was being tracked as a rare individual?” Nemi asked from Memory's bench.

“Yes.”

“Did you tell him?”

“No. He was a child.”

“Did you tell Sava?”

“I had no permission.”

“Did you oppose using him?”

With her lateral eyes, Mera searched for a point no longer in the hall.

“I opposed authority over others. I recommended technical work, emergency teams, environments where delayed flight could save lives. I thought I was offering him a way to exist without being fully normalised.”

“You sent him towards dangerous places.”

“Yes.”

“For his good or the system's?”

“At the time, I didn't separate the questions.”

Taren watched from the back row. He had not spoken to Mera since leaving for Vaor. After the hearing, he waited for her in the corridor.

“You knew,” he said.

“I knew part of it.”

“When you told me my difference could be useful, you weren't trying to help me. You were preparing me.”

“I was trying to do both.”

“You can't.”

“Parents do. Teachers do. You do it with Sen.”

Taren turned his body face-on. Mera did not retreat.

“I tell him who I am,” he said.

“You tell him who you want him not to become. That's an equally powerful form of shaping.”

Taren left without saying goodbye. That evening, he asked Sen to choose for himself whether to keep attending training. The boy asked whether refusal would change how he looked at him. Taren could not promise it would not.

The fourth month concerned the White Winter. Maps showed the mirrors moving in small steps, each justified by protection of central crops. Engineers demonstrated that no individual command had created the catastrophe. The catastrophe emerged through accumulation and because affected regions had no key to challenge it.

A refugee delegation brought containers of frozen soil. They placed them before the tribunal and recited the names of children who had not reached Vaor. At the eighty-third name, the central magistrate requested a break.

“How much of this is evidence and how much ritual?” Seln's lawyer asked.

“Your legal system was born from rituals it learned to call procedure,” Nemi replied.

Sava asked her to stop intervening from the floor. Nemi accepted and published the observation in the trial archive.

The fifth month concerned the Gardens of Kinship. Testimonies contradicted one another. Some described detention, threats and blocked messages. Others described the only place their children had received food during the wars. Hale said the gate had been a prison and his home had nevertheless been there.

“What punishment do you ask for?” the Vaor magistrate asked him.

“I don't know.”

“You're the witness to the harm.”

“Harm doesn't make me an expert in the future.”

Seln looked at him with interest. After the hearing, he sent him a note on administering mixed institutions. Hale deleted it unread, then retrieved it from the archive that same night.

In the sixth month, the prosecution called central agents. One commander admitted closing the Gardens' gates on the Custodian's orders. Another had told staff the families were there voluntarily. A third had refused to flood the pipes where Nemi hid, although regulations permitted it.

“Why did you refuse?” Sava asked.

“The order said capture, not killing.”

“Did the Custodian tell you that?”

“No. I read the limit in the protocol.”

The defence used the testimony to show the system contained moral restraints. The prosecution used it to show that an agent without political office could interpret better than the ruler. Both readings were true.

In the seventh month, Taren was called. The hall filled. Those from Vaor awaited condemnation of the past; the Faces awaited justification of the present. He left his weapon at the entrance and sat without insignia.

“Do you consider that Seln Aru created you?” the prosecution asked.

“No.”

“He tracked you, steered you towards Vaor and administered the conditions in which your unit emerged.”

“He tried to use me. Daran tried to use me. The Houses tried. Sometimes they succeeded. That doesn't make them the authors of every choice.”

“Then what do you accuse him of?”

Taren looked at Seln.

“The things he did and those he concealed so he couldn't be stopped. Not me.”

The defence asked him about Ivarra.

“Did you refuse to stop the air to House Tel, although it would have shortened the fight?”

“Yes.”

“And more than three hundred of your people died.”

“Yes.”

“You decided their lives could be spent for your principle.”

Taren stayed silent.

“Answer,” the magistrate demanded.

“Yes.”

The Faces shifted uneasily in the hall.

“What's the difference between that decision and moving the light to protect the centre?” the lawyer asked.

“My people knew the order and could refuse.”

“Could they? You were the First Face, the city was burning and their comrades were going in.”

Taren remembered the fighter who lowered his weapon only because he stood before him.

“Not as much as I'd have liked to believe,” he said.

The answer weakened the prosecution and strengthened the trial. Sava hated him for it and respected him.

In the eighth month, the panel tried to write the verdict. It could not agree on intentional homicide. Seln had not wanted the deaths in Vaor, but had accepted a risk unknown to the victims. Moving the mirrors had saved crops and killed refugees. The Gardens had protected and imprisoned.

Nemi proposed preserving the disagreements in the verdict. The central magistrate said a sentence could not have five versions.

“Then it will lie in the name of clarity,” she replied.

In the end, every charge received a majority and minority opinion. The public could read both. No one was satisfied. After nine months, the hall was ready for a conclusion that could repair nothing.

“I administered consequences,” he told the tribunal. “Some were produced by my orders. Others would have existed without them. You want a line between them because only then does punishment seem mathematical.”

Sava presided over the civil panel and hated him for understanding judgement's weakness so well.

“You used the population as a means,” she said.

“Every policy uses the population as a means to the population's survival.”

“You hid the choice.”

“Yes. That's the correct accusation.”

When Sava pronounced the conviction, a group from Vaor shouted that the punishment was too small. The old agents called it revenge. The Gardens' families split within the same row: some applauded, others asked to be allowed to visit him.

Taren did not react. Sen, beside Vessa, asked why lifelong detention was not called slow killing. Vessa told him societies sometimes preserved bodies because they did not know what to do with their meaning.

“Then why don't they send him into the Night?” he asked.

“Because that would be an execution with weather as the weapon.”

“But that's what he did to the west.”

“Being able to construct the symmetry doesn't oblige you to use it.”

Sen seemed to understand and not accept. At nine, the difference was still visible.

The tribunal acquitted him of intentional homicide and convicted him of detention, infrastructure manipulation without public oversight and falsification of risk registers. The sentence was lifelong detention in the Gardens of Kinship, with limited archive access.

“You kept the cage,” Sava told him after the verdict.

“Not I.”

“I voted for it.”

“Then you've discovered victory's first rule: the opponent's institutions suddenly become too useful to destroy.”

Seln accepted his sentence without appeal. Over the following years, he sent unsolicited comments on the new constitution from the Gardens. Nemi kept them all. Taren claimed not to read them and sometimes quoted formulations only Seln had used.

Seln was taken back to the same home. The Gardens' gates remained open for everyone else, but became opaque when he approached. He could walk through orchards, speak with visitors and work on unclassified archives. He could not transmit directly to institutions; every text passed through Memory.

In the first week, Nemi received two hundred pages of constitutional commentary. She labelled them *opinions of a prisoner with relevant expertise* and published them alongside survivors' criticisms. Some proposals were adopted. Every adoption provoked a scandal.

“You're letting him rule from his cell,” a Vaor representative said.

“An idea doesn't become untrue because its author is sentenced,” Nemi replied.

“Nor innocent.”

“That's why we keep the name beside it.”

Taren read the comments at night. He marked useful passages and rephrased them in meetings. One day Nemi showed him two almost identical formulations.

“I don't want to give him authority,” he said.

“Then you quote him without letting him carry it.”

“Do you want me to thank him?”

“I want you to know where your instruments come from.”

From then on, Taren published his sources. His popularity did not fall. Many considered the gesture proof he could use even the enemy's mind.

• • •

The Council of Five Nodes worked for three months.

The Chamber of Measure wanted birth limits restored. The Hands Network demanded food for destroyed regions. Memory refused to approve any decision whose data were not public. The civil node fragmented among refugees, families and central cities. Taren's Custodianship could execute orders but could not produce them alone.

In the fourth month, House Orm attacked Tel for control of a thermal reactor.

The Council debated for two days. On the third, the reactor exploded.

Taren declared a continuity emergency and intervened without three keys. He stopped the war in six hours. Four hundred fighters died. Two hundred thousand civilians kept their heat.

The emergency was to expire after ten days.

On the ninth day, House Selc was blocking a food channel. On the seventeenth, a Corps without a House occupied a central city. On the thirty-second, young Tel people killed their own civil representative.

The emergency did not expire.

The new constitution, called the Law of the Living, retained the five nodes but granted the Custodianship direct command in armed conflicts, ecological disasters and threats to infrastructure. The threat list initially occupied one page. After five years, it occupied eighteen.

The Orm–Tel reactor conflict began as a maintenance dispute. The reactor heated three cities and two agricultural networks. Orm had built it, Tel controlled the fuel tunnels, and the new Hands Network maintained that vital infrastructure could no longer belong to a House. Every side had documents, dead people and a story about theft.

On the first day, the Council of Five Nodes discussed jurisdiction. The Chamber of Measure said the reactor should be administered according to its dependent population. The Hands demanded technical control. Memory refused transfer until contract archives were published. The civil node had two delegations challenging each other. Taren requested permission to separate the armed forces in the area. He did not receive the three keys.

On the second day, Orm stopped a line for “inspection”. Tel interpreted the act as a blockade and occupied the fuel chamber. The Council requested observers. The observers were stopped at the gate.

Taren wanted to intervene. Sava voted against.

“We can't turn every unresolved dispute into a Custodianship operation,” she said.

“There are weapons beside a reactor.”

“Then we enforce disarmament through sanctions.”

“How long does that take?”

“Two days.”

“We don't have two days.”

“That's exactly the sentence you'll bypass every node with.”

Taren withdrew his request. The following night, a Tel group tried to take over the cooling system. No one intended to blow up the reactor. A door closed over cables, pumps switched to secondary power, and a projectile struck a gas reservoir. The explosion was visible from three cities.

Taren declared the emergency before the map finished its estimate. This time he did not ask for the keys. The Custodianship entered, separated the forces and occupied the remaining reactor. It stopped the conflict in six hours.

Afterwards, Sava visited the field morgue. Four hundred fighters had died, mostly in the explosion. Among them were technicians who had tried to cool the installation. The survivors called Taren their saviour. Two hundred thousand civilians had kept their heat.

“I was wrong,” she told him.

Taren stood beside a cracked reservoir, still in armour.

“Yes.”

“We shouldn't have waited.”

“No.”

“Don't make it a general rule.”

He looked at her wearily.

“You just saw four hundred bodies.”

“And I know how easily they'll become an argument for the next intervention, when the risk won't be the same.”

“How do you want me to lead?”

“Remembering that sometimes you're right, and that's the most dangerous part.”

The emergency was to expire after ten days. On the ninth, House Selc blocked a food channel. Taren requested an extension. Three nodes approved. On the seventeenth, the Corps without a House occupied a central city. On the thirty-second, Tel's civil representative was killed. Every incident was real. Each made returning to normal rules seem irresponsible.

The threat list was written in haste: armed conflicts, infrastructure attacks, ecological disasters. Then came information sabotage, uncoordinated migration, refusal of food quotas, epidemics, transport blockages, weapons proliferation. No one introduced an article without being able to point to a crisis that justified it.

Nemi kept the originating event's name beside every category. She had hoped this would keep exceptions tied to circumstances. Instead, the names became precedents.

Taren banned the Houses’ hereditary titles. In their place, Face ranks were awarded through service and examination. Commanders’ children, however, had access to the best schools, better food and instructors. They passed the examinations three times as often as everyone else. The caste the law rejected emerged through training.

Additional reproductive rights were abolished as a privilege of rank. They were reintroduced as compensation for risk.

Vessa wrote the medical criteria. She tried to exclude selection for aggression, limit the number of embryos and publish the side effects. Private Houses opened clinics outside the register. Families sought the same combinations: a slow alarm response, bone density, early fertility, shorter auditory sails. Within two generations, Taren’s markers became eleven times more common among the Faces.

“I am trying to reduce the harm,” Vessa told Sava.

“I know.”

“That is how every serious complicity begins.”

Sava did not contradict her. She herself led the reorganised Chamber of Measure. She had eliminated postponements beyond fertile age, introduced transparent lotteries and guaranteed everyone a first birth. The population grew by thirteen per cent, and rations fell. After the first poor harvest, she was forced to reintroduce restrictions on second births.

A woman came to her office with an unauthorised pregnancy and asked:

“How much future must we be given before it becomes a form of no?”

It was not the same woman from Ivarra. The question had survived better than the answers.

The abolition of hereditary titles was celebrated in the Low Square. The Houses’ towers lowered their emblems, and the children of former rulers had to sit the same examinations as everyone else. In the first year, the statistics looked promising. In the second, private Face academies appeared in the wealthy districts. They taught frontal orientation, panic control, strategy and command dialects. Their fees exceeded a Hands family’s income.

Sava proposed equivalent public schools. The Custodianship funded them, but the best instructors stayed at the academies, where commanders’ families offered them housing and birth rights. The law had opened the examination. The economy had restricted access to preparation.

Reproductive rights for risk produced the same mutation. Rank privilege had been abolished; medical compensation seemed fairer. A fighter exposed to radiation received additional embryos because his fertility might decline. A family that sent two members on the Margin Campaigns received a compensatory quota. Within five years, military service became the surest path to a large family.

Vessa led the clinical commission. Every form tried to separate healing from selection. She banned selection by aggression score, limited genetic combinations and required the children to be monitored. Private clinics moved into tunnels between jurisdictions. They promised not aggression but “stability under stress”, not a reduced flight reflex but “decision continuity”.

During an inspection, Vessa found twenty-three embryos created from the same Vaor commander’s line. The clinic’s owner claimed the families had chosen them independently.

“They all requested the same traits and the same donor in a single month,” she said.

“Reputation is distributed information.”

“Reputation is an advertisement pretending to be natural selection.”

She closed the clinic. Two weeks later, the families opened another under the protection of a House abolished in law and intact in society.

Sava found her in the laboratory at night, looking at Taren’s markers on a screen. Their frequency had already risen among Face children.

“We can ban the combinations,” Sava said.

“Then they will hide them, and we will no longer be able to track the effects.”

“We can limit the reproductive advantages of service.”

“Then the Custodianship will say you are sending people into danger without compensation.”

“We can shut down the private academies.”

“Then the families will train them at home.”

Vessa switched off the screen.

“There is no single law for us to repair. There are hundreds of parents who have seen what kind of child receives food, songs and a future.”

Sava sat beside her. In the old Chamber, selection had been centralised and explicit. The new order had dispersed it into private choices rewarded in public. It was harder to condemn, and perhaps more powerful.

A few days later, the woman with the unauthorised pregnancy came to the office. She already had one child and had requested the right to a second before the poor harvest. The lottery had rejected her.

“How much future must we be given before it becomes a form of no?” she asked.

Sava could not grant her an exception without taking food from elsewhere. She could not report her without repeating the old Chamber. She offered to register the pregnancy, provide medical support and allocate a quota to be deducted from the community’s following year.

“Then my neighbours pay for the child,” the woman said.

“Yes.”

“And they will tell the child that.”

“Probably.”

“Is this your freedom? I make the choice, and you punish the people around me until they hate me?”

Sava felt that any answer immediately became another institution.

“It is a choice in a world with finite food,” she said.

“The old Chamber said the same thing more prettily.”

The woman left without thanking her. Sava approved the pregnancy and stayed in the office for a long time, listening to the pipes. The Law of the Living was still being drafted. On the walls, the plans promised five nodes, terms of office, transparency and equal rights. In the city, training, food, risk and desire were already writing a constitution harder to read.


CHAPTER XXIII

THE SON WITHOUT FEAR

TAREN AND SEN · TWILIGHT BELT · THE NEXT EIGHT YEARS

The return of peace did not abolish the warriors. It moved them into schools, families, examinations and rituals. In eight years, what had been a rare deviation became the model by which a generation measured its maturity.

Sen joined the Face Corps at nine. Taren had forbidden him to use the family name in the examination. All the examiners recognised him by his burn and by the way he did not flinch.

The examination took place in a room without insignia, below the level of the old Chamber. Candidates entered alone. Their names were concealed, and the assessors saw only their age, medical condition and assigned number. Taren had requested this procedure after Sen announced that he wanted to join the Face junior programme. He said his son had to pass through the same door as everyone else.

On the morning of the examination, half the assessors recognised him by his burn before the door closed. The others recognised him by the way he held his body: facing forward, motionless, his sails trimmed shorter than those of civilian children. At nine, Sen already wore a version of the uniform, although the regulations required neutral clothing.

The first test was the old saryx shadow. The system projected movement, scent and low frequencies; the child had to choose between shelter, warning and confrontation. Most candidates fled first, then corrected their course. Sen stayed. He drew his training blade and struck the point of light where the wing joint would have been.

The assessors noted *rapid decision under stress*. None wrote that the boy had seen a real saryx kill Raal, and that his response might be memory, not aptitude.

The second test placed three doors and a group of virtual children before him. Behind one was an attacker; behind another, a fire; the third led to a safe tunnel too narrow for everyone. There was no complete solution. The examination measured the order in which the candidate asked questions, not only the choice.

Sen requested access to the ventilation system, the exact number of children and the time before the fire spread. When the assessor said the data was unavailable, he asked who had withheld it.

“That is not relevant,” replied the woman behind the screen.

“Then the attacker may be the institution that designed the test.”

A laugh in the room was quickly stifled. Sen chose the safe tunnel, divided the group by body size and stayed behind the others. Two virtual children died. He asked to repeat the test with a different distribution.

“The test is over,” the assessor said.

“Then you do not find out whether I have learned.”

At the end, he received one of the highest scores in the cohort. Taren read the report and noticed the comments: *command profile*, *tolerance of loss*, *spontaneous identification with responsibility*. No paragraph mentioned his name. In the confidential appendix, however, appeared: *probable continuity of the Ilen line; high symbolic value.*

“Invalidate the examination,” Taren told the director.

“We cannot invalidate a performance because the assessors know who the candidate is.”

“Then it was not anonymous.”

“No procedure can make society forget a scar.”

Sen was listening beside the door.

“Did I pass?” he asked.

Taren turned towards him.

“You passed a test designed by people who wanted to see you pass.”

“That is not my fault.”

“No.”

“Then will you let me?”

Taren looked at the small uniform, the burn and Raal’s necklace beneath the collar.

“Yes.”

Sen smiled. For him, all his father’s warnings disappeared into that word.

Vessa was waiting outside. She congratulated him and asked him to come for a medical examination the following week. When the boy left to join his classmates, she turned to Taren.

“You tried to remove his name from the examination and left him his statue,” she said.

“He has no statue.”

“He has a living one. It walks through the city and leads the Custodianship.”

“What should I have done? Forbidden him?”

“No. You should have stopped pretending that an equal door corrects the different roads leading to it.”

Taren did not answer. In the years that followed, he would repeat that Sen had earned every rank. It was true. It was not the whole truth.

• • •

The junior programme combined schooling with service. The children learned ecology, repairs, history and bodily control. Each week, they spent a cycle in civilian institutions: warehouses, clinics, archives, farms. The idea had been Sava’s. She wanted the Faces to understand that the world did not begin at the edge of a weapon.

In his first year, Sen worked at a moss farm in Ivarra. His task was to clean the fine channels without breaking the nutrient filaments. He was slow and bad at it. A Hands girl, Deli, showed him how to feel the pressure with his fingers.

“You do not push everything that refuses to move,” she said.

“I am not pushing.”

“The channel says otherwise.”

He tried again. By the end of the day, he was breaking fewer roots than that morning. The instructor gave him an average score. In the public report, the episode appeared as evidence of the Custodian’s son’s humility. Deli was not mentioned.

“Tell them she taught me,” Sen insisted.

“We do not control the reports,” the instructor replied.

Sen went to Memory and submitted the correction. Nemi accepted it. The recording became popular: the young Face acknowledging his Hands teacher. Deli received an invitation to the academy, but her family needed her at the farm, and she declined. Sen did not find out until years later. In the story, equality had worked.

At twelve, he was sent on a transport patrol, officially as an observer. Nivari matured quickly, and the crisis generations had driven the age of service lower still. Sen’s body was almost adult; his judgement was not.

The convoy was taking food to an unregistered community at the edge of Night. The locals had stolen two wagons the previous season, and the Custodianship had classified them as a risk. The patrol commander, Varo, wanted to retain half the rations until the community handed over the thieves.

Sen read the manifest. The children were already receiving less than they needed.

“The Code forbids using food against civilians,” he said.

“The Code forbids collective punishment,” Varo replied. “This is a logistical guarantee.”

“What is the difference for those who do not eat?”

“The intention and the condition for restitution.”

Sen opened the Custodianship channel. Varo shut down his terminal.

“You are an observer.”

“I can report the violation.”

“You can report it after the mission.”

In the settlement, people emerged from the tunnels, thin and armed with tools. Varo deployed his Faces. An old man said the thieves had died in the blizzard. He had no bodies; edge hunters had eaten them.

“Then the debt remains with the community,” Varo said.

Sen saw the children watching the wagons. He remembered the White Winter camps, which he knew only from recordings and adults’ silences.

“I will guarantee the shipment,” he said.

Varo laughed.

“With what?”

“My family quota and my service pay until the loss is covered.”

“It is not enough.”

“Then record that you refused a guarantee and kept the food.”

Varo looked at him for a long time. He did not fear the boy, but he feared the name the anonymous procedure had been unable to erase. He unloaded every wagon.

The report praised Sen for “mediation”. Varo received a reprimand and was promoted the following year. The community repaid a third of the food; the rest was no longer demanded. Sen learned that the law could protect the weak when someone powerful spoke it. He did not learn how the same law sounded in the mouth of someone without a name.

At fourteen, he led short patrols. He was rigorous. He shared his food equally, slept last and forbade trophies. When a member of his unit struck a prisoner, he removed him from service even though he was the son of an influential House. The story spread. The young Faces loved him not for his gentleness, but because his rule could humble anyone.

That same year, he met Deli again. She now led a repair team and had two fingers deformed by an untreated infection. The academy had offered medical access with its invitation, but she had lost that right when her family declined.

“You could have come,” Sen said.

“And who would have cleaned the channels?”

“They would have found someone else.”

“Exactly.”

He did not understand at once. To him, being replaceable was an insult. To Deli, it was the reason institutions could demand sacrifice without counting it.

Sen secured her operation through the Custodianship fund. She accepted, then refused the administrative position she was offered.

“I do not want to be your good story,” she said.

“It is not a story. It is treatment.”

“Then let it remain that.”

At her request, Nemi kept the incident only in the medical file. It was one of the rare occasions when the archive became truer through what it refused to turn into a symbol.

At fifteen, Sen killed his first rebel commander. The revolt had taken place in a thermal city at the edge of Day. The Hands had seized the facility after the Face administrators reduced the water quotas. The Custodianship had been negotiating for two weeks. In the third, the rebels captured a train and killed two guards.

Sen led the team that entered through the cooling pipe. He found the commander, a woman named Iara, beside the reactor panel. Her weapon was lowered. Behind her, a device was counting down to overheating.

“Stop it,” Sen said.

“Turn on the district’s water.”

“I cannot do that from here.”

“Then call someone who can.”

Time was running out. Sen could shoot her hand, but Iara held the touch control on the detonator. He could negotiate and risk the reactor. He shot her in the head. Her body fell without releasing the mechanism. A technician stopped the sequence seven seconds before failure.

The Custodianship declared the intervention exemplary. The city received water three days later, following an investigation that confirmed the cuts had been excessive. Sen attended Iara’s funeral without insignia. Her family asked him to leave.

“I stopped the reactor,” he told Vessa after returning.

“Yes.”

“If I had waited, thousands would have died.”

“Perhaps.”

“Do not talk like Nemi.”

“What do you want me to say?”

“That I did the right thing.”

Vessa examined his pupils and pulse. The boy had not slept for two cycles.

“You made a defensible choice in a situation badly constructed by many people. If we simply call it good, everyone else disappears.”

“Father would have fired.”

“Probably.”

“Then why do you tell him he saved the world?”

“I do not tell him that.”

Sen left angry. That night, he watched the recording nine times. Not the shot, but the seven seconds afterwards, when the technician ran towards the panel. The next day, he asked for the report to mention the technician’s name. The technician became known. Iara remained “rebel commander”.

At sixteen, Sen had three children with two partners. Nivar biology made early maturity possible, and Face culture turned it into a duty. The families had chosen the embryos for compatibility, health and the same traits the clinics called stability. Sen had said he did not want selection. Yet he had accepted the variants recommended by the doctors, and those recommendations had been built from his generation’s success.

Vessa examined the children. One had a minor hearing malformation associated with the combination of bone-density markers. Another did not react to alarm sounds and was already being praised for calmness.

“Do not test him like a Face,” she told the parents.

“We are not testing him,” one of the partners replied. “Only observing.”

“Observation changes the way you touch him.”

“And you observe him medically.”

Vessa had no clean answer. Medicine and culture looked at the same child with different intentions, and both shaped him.

“We are losing him,” she told Taren.

“He was never ours.”

“Not as a son. As a possibility.”

Sen believed in the Code. He recited it perfectly, punished those who killed after surrender and divided the patrol’s food equally. He also believed the Faces were the world’s only true adults, because only they accepted risk. When a civilian representative contradicted him, he listened face-on, then carried out the Custodianship’s order.

He was more disciplined than Taren, less cruel in his gestures and far more dangerous in his convictions.

Taren heard the conversation about possibility one evening when Sen had not come home. “Home” was an apartment beneath the tower, chosen to demonstrate modesty and guarded by two corridors of agents. Vessa lived there only sometimes. Their relationship had never acquired a stable name. It had been closeness in crisis, then family, then managed disagreement.

“What did you want him to be?” Taren asked.

“A person who could refuse the role without feeling he was betraying us.”

“He can refuse it.”

“At what price?”

“Every choice has a price.”

“Not every choice has statues of its father in the square.”

Taren sat down. Exhaustion stiffened his movements. His heart had begun to lose its rhythm, and the stimulants used in the war had scarred the muscle. Vessa had prescribed rest. The Custodianship continually prescribed another emergency.

“I did not ask for the statue.”

“You refused three times to take it down and called your refusal respect for the public vote.”

“If I take it down, it becomes more important.”

“Yes. Symbols are unfair that way. You still have to choose which unfairness you sustain.”

Taren looked at Sen’s room. On the wall were maps, the Code and Raal’s necklace. No toys remained.

“Perhaps I lost him before I understood that I was holding him,” he said.

Vessa folded her sails.

“He is not lost. He is simply very good at what the world asks of him.”

This did not reassure him.

• • •

In the eighth year of the new order, the Festival of Retreat was restored.

The name remained, but the ritual changed. Mechanical shadows crossed the Low Square, and children no longer had to run towards the markings. They had to stay in the circle until they identified the trajectory, then decide whether shelter, warning or confrontation was the correct response. The exercise was presented as adaptive freedom.

In practice, the crowd applauded only the children who stayed.

Sava stood on the same terrace from which she had seen the first saryx. Beside her, Nemi was recording. She was twenty-two, an age at which many nivari began to grow old, but the Memory Corps had replaced her damaged joints and prolonged her hearing with implants.

“Will the archive call this a return to tradition?” Sava asked.

“The archive will preserve both versions of the ritual.”

“And the one that will be sung?”

“We do not control the songs.”

“No one controls the most effective things.”

On the platform, a small child froze beneath the shadow. He had not chosen to stay; his body had locked. The crowd applauded anyway. The instructor drew the Face sign on his chest.

“They no longer even distinguish courage from the old reflex,” Sava said.

“Perhaps they were never so different.”

The restored festival drew people from across the Belt. The square had been enlarged and covered with a transparent membrane. Above it, the red sky seemed close enough for the children to touch. Stalls sold model saryxes, toy blades and bands playing the First Face rhythm. The old Retreat figurines, in which the ancestors ran for shelter, could be found only in antique shops.

Nemi recorded from the Memory terrace. Beside her, Sava wore a plain cloak. She had relinquished the Chamber’s daily management, but remained on the Council and was summoned whenever a quota caused protests. She had aged; her hind joints had been partly replaced, and the down at her temples had turned white.

Sen stood on the platform beside the instructors. His three children were in the families’ area, too young for the test. The crowd watched them almost as much as the participants. When Sen raised his hand, the square fell silent before the loudspeakers amplified the gesture.

The child who froze beneath the shadow was named Ari. Sava saw his body lock: eyes wide, limbs drawn in, breathing stopped. It was not resolve. It was the old freezing reflex that Taren’s therapy had once tried to correct. The instructor drew the Face sign, and his family wept with pride.

After the ceremony, Sava found them backstage. Ari was trembling.

“Do you want to join the programme?” she asked him.

The child looked at his parents.

“Yes.”

“What did you feel beneath the shadow?”

“I stayed.”

“Did you choose?”

His mother intervened.

“He did what he was supposed to. Why are you confusing him?”

“Because he will be trained on the basis of the reason we give him now.”

His father took Ari by the shoulder.

“Before, children like him were treated. Now they are seen.”

Sava recognised the truth. The old order had turned difference into a defect. The new order turned it into destiny. Between them, the child had little room left to discover what he had been.

Nemi kept both the applause and the backstage trembling in the archive. The public version of the festival used the image of the sign being drawn on his chest. The backstage image was accessed by seventy-three people in its first year.

That evening, the songs called Ari “the one who chose before he knew the words”.

• • •

After the ceremony, Sava found Taren at the culture growing over Oru’s grave. The roots produced bitter fruit, rich in minerals. Taren was thirty-four and already struggled to move. Wounds, implants and years of veyrin in childhood had shortened his life.

“The towers can be seen from Ivarra,” Sava said.

“That is what they were built for.”

“Daran would be pleased.”

“Daran was never pleased. That is why he understood politics.”

They sat on the ground. Above them, Oru’s root pumped water.

“You gave a generation meaning,” Sava said.

“That sounds like an accusation.”

“It is. Your meaning always requires someone to stand outside it. The soft. The Protected. Those who did not pass the examination. You took their peace and gave them roles written by others.”

“Your peace gave them births written by others.”

“Mine?”

“Ours. Stop hiding only in the part of the system you tried to repair.”

Sava touched the soil.

“Oru would say we replaced a simplified ecosystem with one full of predators we pretend to control.”

“Oru brought back the edge hunters.”

“Sterile ones.”

“Nothing remains culturally sterile.”

Taren plucked a fruit and bit it. Its bitterness tightened his face.

“Before,” he continued, “we were told that life required continuity. It was enough not to die, not to have too many children and to leave the world unchanged. When the crises came, some discovered they could be necessary. You cannot ask them to forget that.”

“No. But you can refuse to build a world that produces crises so they remain necessary.”

Taren looked at the towers on the horizon.

“Do you think I am doing that?”

“Not consciously yet.”

“And Sen?”

Sava did not answer at once.

“Sen will not need to lie about it.”

When she left, Taren stayed beside the root. For the first time in many years, he heard three knocks from the depths. They were not a military signal. The old pipes were contracting with the change in temperature, exactly as Sava had tried to explain on the night of the Festival.

Taren smiled.

Some warnings were only noises. Some noises became warnings because someone needed them.

Sava had found Taren at Oru’s root after the festival. The culture had grown around the remains and now supplied a small water station. The neighbourhood children called it the Hero’s Pump, although Oru had never officially been declared a hero. The name had emerged from the need to place one dead person beside the others.

As they spoke, Taren rubbed his chest. Sava noticed and did not interrupt. She had learned that medical questions made him lie.

When they came to Sen, the silence between them lasted as long as a train passing through the roots below.

“You can no longer guide him through warnings alone,” Sava said.

“He does not listen to me as a son either. He assesses me as a predecessor.”

“Then talk to him about the things you have not resolved.”

“He sees them.”

“He sees the institutions. He does not see you afraid.”

Taren gave a short laugh.

“Everyone knows I am afraid.”

“They know you act despite it. They do not know why you wake at night, which people you cannot look at, how often you wanted to run and stayed only because the others were watching.”

“Those things do not help a Custodian.”

“They help a son understand that the office is not a species.”

Taren plucked a second bitter fruit and did not eat it. He placed it beside the root.

“Tomorrow,” he said.

Sava knew how often the near future became the place where leaders stored their private lives.

As she left, she heard the pipes contracting. Taren stayed alone and smiled at the noise that had triggered his first false warning in childhood.

That night, he sent Sen a simple message: *Come in the morning. Without your uniform.*

Sen read it during a patrol meeting. He intended to go. Before dawn, however, a pipe burst in a frontier town, and the Custodianship asked him to lead the evacuation. Taren approved the mission. Neither proposed postponing the order for the conversation.

He died in his sleep thirty-nine days later.

There was no enemy, wound or last command. His heart, subjected to years of stimulants and alarms, stopped in a guarded room.

Vessa found him. The cardiac monitor had sent the alarm, but the guards had assumed it was one of the frequent errors. Taren lay on his side, one hand beneath his head. He wore no cloak and had not reached his weapon. On the table lay the bitter fruit brought from Oru’s root and a list of subjects for Sen: Raal, the fear in the tunnel, the Tel dead, the keys, what to do when the Code became a tactical advantage for the enemy.

Vessa touched him, then called the doctors, though she knew. After the room filled, she took the list and put it in her pocket. She did not want Memory’s first act to turn the unfinished conversation into a perfect testament.

Sen arrived six hours later. He still wore the dust of the evacuation. Vessa told him his father had died in his sleep. He asked whether he had been poisoned, whether there had been an attack, whether there was a final order. Every question sought an adversary or a task.

“His heart stopped,” Vessa said. “That is all.”

“That cannot be all.”

“Bodies are not obliged to offer an ending suited to a life.”

Sen went in alone. He stood beside the bed for a long time. When he came out, he had not cried.

“What was he going to tell me?” he asked.

Vessa touched the list in her pocket.

“I do not know.”

It was the first lie she had told him about Taren. She kept it for three days, until after the funeral, so that the boy would have at least one interval in which his father could not be reduced to instructions. Then she gave him the sheet.

Sen read it and folded it.

“He did not finish.”

“No.”

“Then I will finish it.”

“That was not the conversation.”

“How do you know?”

Vessa saw how quickly grief became a mandate.

“Because he asked you to come without your uniform.”

Sen placed the sheet beneath Raal’s necklace.

The funeral rites lasted three days. The abolished Houses came beneath their old emblems. The Hands carried tools, Memory projected contradictory fragments, and the Faces beat the return rhythm so loudly that dust fell from the ceiling. Sava had asked for Oru’s death, the refusal to cut Tel’s air and the votes against Taren to be included. The ceremonial committee considered them inappropriate political details.

Nevertheless, Nemi projected the complete chronology onto a side wall. Most people watched the body raised above the square.

The election of the new Custodian began the next day. There were five candidates: two mature commanders, a Hands representative, an archivist and Sen. His age provoked opposition, but the Code set no threshold for anyone who had completed service and the higher examination. His supporters said no one knew the Custodianship better. His opponents said that was precisely the problem.

The civilian node held public hearings. Sen was asked what he would do if three nodes refused him permission to intervene in a crisis.

“I would publish the data and ask again,” he replied.

“And if there is no time?”

“I would intervene on my own responsibility and accept judgement afterwards.”

The crowd applauded. The Hands representative had answered that she would respect the refusal until the procedure produced a different outcome. Her answer was more constitutional and sounded less like protection.

Sen was elected Custodian by four votes out of five.

The fifth node, the civilian one, voted against him.

The vote was not mentioned in the ceremony’s songs.
